### DS-POW-7 — `POWER — blocs, coalitions, and the divided court` · `worldState.politicsLedgers[cid].blocs[]{members,glue,end,strain,covert}`
> *who is standing with whom inside the hall, what holds them, and what breaks them*

**RECEIPT:** `src/domain/worldPulse/settlementPolitics.js:57-61` (Bloc typedef), `:77-148` (caps, floors, dwell), `:159-164` (the dormancy gate), `:309-314` (tie vocabulary), `:414-447` (`classifyGlue`), `:452-464` (`ARCHETYPE_END`, `GOAL_END_HINT`), `:668-669` (receipt kinds), `:746-946` (the authored detail lines)
**STATE-KEY:** `blocs[]{members[], glue{type, detail}, end, strain 0..1,
sinceTick, covert?}` — a CONDITIONALLY MATERIALIZED ledger, absent while the
layer is dormant · `rulingBlocOf → {bloc, consolidation}` (needs ≥ a substantial
share of settlement power AND the governing faction inside) ·
`coalitionConsolidation01` (0 = a fully divided court) · glue types (`compromise`
· `threat` · `doctrine` · `patronage` · `concession`) · ends (`seats` ·
`doctrine` · `commerce` · `survival` · `patron`) · transition receipt kinds
(`formed` · `realigned` · `fractured` · `exposed` · `deferred`) · leader-tie
vocabulary (warm: family, mentor_student, ally, respect, lover, patron_client;
hostile: rival, enemy, hard-blocking at bitter/mortal/personal/serious).
**SLOTS:** `{settlement}` `{faction}` `{counterpart}` `{npc}` `{seat}` `{reason}` `{band}` `{timeband_since}`
**SECTION-TARGET:** `power`
**PROVENANCE + FENCE (the dormancy gate is the whole of it).** The layer lights
only when settlement politics AND faction competition are both enabled.
**Dormant means no bloc, factor 1.0, consolidation 0 — and the prose must
render a plain undifferentiated court, never invent a coalition.** This is the
single most likely mis-wiring in the shape and the reason the dormant cells are
authored first. Where the ledger IS live, the glue's `detail` is an authored
recorded cause and licenses a full causal clause; the `strain` scalar does not
print. `patronage` is PEOPLE-HELD and dies at succession — that entailment is
the glue's own definition and is lawful to state. `compromise` is covert and
catastrophic on exposure; under an autarchy an opposition bloc forms covert
outright, and both are `dm-only`. The bloc cap is three per settlement and the
`deferred` receipt is what a fourth alignment produces — a real recorded gate
and therefore a `[counterforce]` cell.
**PDF PARITY:** parity for the overt cells; `PRINT-DEFERRED` for every `dm-only`
cell in this shape.

**layer DORMANT (no ledger materialized)**
1. `[ledger]` The hall at {settlement} is not organized into sides. The factions sit as factions, and nothing binds any two of them into anything larger.
2. `[street]` There are interests at {settlement} and there are no camps; a question is answered by whoever cares about that question.
3. `[visitor]` A stranger at {settlement} cannot work out who is aligned with whom, and the reason is that nobody is.

**consolidation 0 — a fully divided court (live layer, no ruling bloc)**
1. `[ledger]` No combination at {settlement} holds enough of the hall to govern it; every decision must be assembled separately, and several are not assembled at all.
2. `[unfolding · dm-only]` The court at {settlement} is divided finely enough that it is cheap to influence — a small interest buys a great deal here, because there is no majority for it to have to outbid.
3. `[street]` The town waits longer for answers than a town this size should, and the waiting is arithmetic rather than idleness.

**a RULING bloc, consolidated**
1. `[ledger]` A working majority sits at {settlement} with the {seat} inside it; what the hall decides is decided by that combination and announced by the {seat}.
2. `[street]` The town knows which houses go together at {settlement} and takes its business to whichever of them is nearest.
3. `[counterforce]` There are interests at {settlement} outside the governing combination with real weight, and they do not move — outbidding a settled majority costs more than any single question is worth.

**glue `concession` (seats and revenue traded)**
1. `[ledger]` What holds the combination at {settlement} together is a trade: seats and revenue for a working majority, seat-held rather than personal, and outbiddable by anybody who arrives with more.
2. `[street]` The arrangement in the hall at {settlement} is a business one and is treated as one by everybody in it.
3. `[unfolding]` The majority at {settlement} survives every leader it has and would not survive a better offer, and there is no secret about which risk it is running.

**glue `patronage` (personal loyalty between leaders)**
1. `[ledger]` The combination at {settlement} is held by a personal loyalty between the people at the head of it — strong while those hands hold those seats, and worth nothing the moment either changes.
2. `[street]` The alliance in the hall at {settlement} is between two people rather than two houses, and the town has noticed which.
3. `[unfolding]` What binds the majority at {settlement} will die with its bearers' tenure rather than with the arrangement, and the succession that ends it has not yet been called.

**glue `doctrine` (a shared creed)**
1. `[ledger]` The seats that hold together at {settlement} hold together on a shared creed; it is the most durable binding in the hall and the least negotiable.
2. `[street]` The houses that agree at {settlement} agree about something larger than the town, which makes them very hard to divide over anything smaller.
3. `[counterforce]` The combination at {settlement} has been offered better terms than it holds and declined them; a binding of belief is not outbid by a binding of coin.

**glue `threat` (a common danger at the walls)**
1. `[ledger]` The fractious have closed ranks at {settlement} because of what is at the walls; the combination is real, effective and entirely contingent on the danger continuing.
2. `[unfolding]` What holds the hall at {settlement} together is outside it, and will leave when the danger does.
3. `[counterforce]` Houses at {settlement} that have not agreed on anything in years are agreeing now, and none of them mistakes it for a reconciliation.

**glue `compromise` (a corruption leash)** *(every variant `dm-only`)*
1. `[ledger · dm-only]` One of the seats in the combination at {settlement} carries a leash, and the combination does not name it aloud — it is the strongest binding in the hall and the only one that shatters rather than loosens.
2. `[unfolding · dm-only]` What holds the majority at {settlement} together would end it in a single afternoon if it were said out loud, and everybody inside it is arranging their affairs accordingly.
3. `[street · dm-only]` The hall at {settlement} agrees on more than it should and nobody outside it can account for why.

**end `seats`**
1. `[ledger]` What the combination at {settlement} wants is office. Everything else it takes up is taken up for the effect on who holds what.
2. `[street]` The combination at {settlement} takes an interest in everything and cares about one thing, and the one thing is who is sitting where.
3. `[unfolding]` What the combination at {settlement} is assembling toward is a hall it does not have to assemble anything for, and each season's trade moves it a little nearer.

**end `commerce`**
1. `[ledger]` The combination at {settlement} is organized around money, and its politics are legible the moment the question is framed as a cost.
2. `[street]` The houses that agree at {settlement} agree about money, and the agreement lasts exactly as far as the money does.
3. `[visitor]` A stranger who wants something from the combination at {settlement} is not asked what he believes, only what he is offering.

**end `doctrine`**
1. `[ledger]` The combination at {settlement} is organized around a belief and will spend office to serve it, which its rivals consistently fail to price correctly.
2. `[street]` The combination at {settlement} will not trade the thing it is about, which its rivals keep discovering after they have made the offer.
3. `[counterforce]` There are seats the combination at {settlement} could take and will not, because taking them would mean conceding the point the combination exists to hold.

**end `survival`**
1. `[ledger]` The combination at {settlement} has one purpose and it is getting the town through; anything not bearing on that is being deferred and openly so.
2. `[street]` Everything the combination at {settlement} is doing is aimed at the town still being here afterwards, and it says so plainly.
3. `[unfolding]` The combination at {settlement} is spending its own future to buy the town a present one, and every member of it knows the bill is coming.

**end `patron`**
1. `[ledger · dm-only]` What the combination at {settlement} serves is somebody's interest rather than the town's, and the somebody is not always in the town.
2. `[street · dm-only]` The combination at {settlement} serves an interest, and the interest is not a faction of this town and is not always in it.
3. `[unfolding · dm-only]` What the combination at {settlement} pursues answers to somebody who never appears in the hall, and the answering is the whole of its consistency.

**receipt `formed`**
1. `[ledger]` Houses at {settlement} that had no arrangement now have one, and the hall's arithmetic changed the day they announced it.
2. `[street]` Two interests at {settlement} started voting together and have not stopped, and the town worked out what that meant before the hall admitted it.
3. `[visitor]` A stranger who dealt with {settlement}'s hall before the season finds two houses now answering as one, and finds nobody willing to explain when that started.

**receipt `realigned`**
1. `[ledger]` The combination at {settlement} is not the combination it was; a seat left and another took its place, and what the majority wants moved with it.
2. `[unfolding]` The alignment at {settlement} has shifted rather than broken, and what it will now agree to is a different list from last season's.
3. `[street]` The combination at {settlement} kept its name and changed its mind, and the town noticed the second before it noticed the first.

**receipt `fractured`**
1. `[ledger]` The combination at {settlement} has come apart. It lost its members and dissolved, and the hall is back to assembling each decision separately.
2. `[street]` The arrangement in the hall at {settlement} ended, and it ended over a burden that fell on one house and not the others — that house is the aggrieved party now, and says so.
3. `[unfolding]` A leader who bound the combination at {settlement} left the seat and the personal loyalty went with them; nothing seat-held remained to hold the rest together.

**receipt `exposed`** *(every variant `dm-only`)*
1. `[ledger · dm-only]` The leash was dragged into the light at {settlement} and the combination shattered in scandal; what had been the hall's most durable arrangement was its most brittle.
2. `[street · dm-only]` Something was uncovered at {settlement} and the hall reorganized itself within the week, and the town has a fairly accurate idea of what.
3. `[unfolding · dm-only]` What came out at {settlement} is still working through the hall; the combination is gone and the people who were in it are not, and the arrangements they still hold are the next problem.

**receipt `deferred` (the alignment exists and has not happened)**
1. `[threshold]` The alignment is there at {settlement} and the moment has not come; two houses need each other and something keeps them apart, and the keeping-apart is entered every time the question is raised.
2. `[counterforce]` {settlement} already holds three combinations, and a fourth alignment is being held at the cap rather than refused — the houses concerned are aligned in everything but form.
3. `[ledger]` {faction} outbid the combination for {counterpart}'s seat at {settlement}, and the alignment that would have formed did not.

**hostile leader tie HARD-BLOCKS an otherwise natural alignment**
1. `[counterforce]` Two houses at {settlement} want the same things and will not sit together; what is between the people at the head of them is bitter enough that the interests do not get a hearing.
2. `[street]` The obvious alliance at {settlement} does not happen, and the town knows exactly whose quarrel is preventing it.
3. `[ledger]` The interests of {faction} and {counterpart} at {settlement} coincide almost exactly and the two do not sit together; the block is personal, and it holds harder than any policy would.

**an opposition bloc forms COVERT under an autarchy** *(every variant `dm-only`)*
1. `[ledger · dm-only]` There is an alignment against the {seat} at {settlement} and it is not a bloc, it is a conspiracy — under this kind of rule there is no other form it could take.
2. `[unfolding · dm-only]` Seats at {settlement} are coordinating where they cannot be seen to, which is slower, smaller and a great deal more dangerous than coordinating openly.
3. `[street · dm-only]` There are meetings at {settlement} that are not meetings, held by people who have no reason to be in the same room and are anyway.

---

### DS-DEF-1 — `Defense › Defensive posture header` · `defenseProfile.readiness + resourceAnalysis.{terrain,strategicValue} + safetyProfile.guardEffectivenessDesc`
> *what the town amounts to as a defended place, before any particular threat is named*

**RECEIPT:** `src/components/new/tabs/DefenseTab.jsx:120-136` · bands `src/domain/display/defenseScoreBands.js:30-39`
**STATE-KEY:** `defenseProfile.readiness.label` (band word) read against
`scoreBand` (`STRONG` ≥65 · `ADEQUATE` ≥40 · `WEAK` ≥20 · `CRITICAL` <20) ·
`resourceAnalysis.terrain` · `resourceAnalysis.strategicValue` ·
`safetyProfile.guardEffectivenessDesc`.
**SLOTS:** `{settlement}` `{route}` `{counterpart}` `{band}`
**SECTION-TARGET:** `defense`
**PROVENANCE + FENCE:** the readiness word is a summary of the five arms and
asserts nothing about any one of them — a `STRONG` header over a `CRITICAL`
food arm is a real and common state, and the header variant may not imply
otherwise. Terrain and strategic value are STANDING facts of the site with no
recorded history; they never license a causal clause about how the town came
to be there. `guardEffectivenessDesc` is engine prose about the watch and
belongs in the public-order shape (`DS-DEF-3`), not here.
**COMPOSITION FENCE (§0h V1-g):** this block's `ADEQUATE` readiness cell and
`DS-DEF-10`'s `ADEQUATE` per-arm badge cell read closely alike, because the two
states genuinely rhyme — a covered-with-no-margin *town* and a covered-with-no-
margin *arm*. They are different fields on different rows and both are kept;
**a composed Defense tab draws one of them, never both.**
**PDF PARITY:** parity.

**readiness `STRONG`**
1. `[visitor]` {settlement} reads as a defended place from the road in — the approaches are watched, the works are kept up, and none of it looks like it was thrown together.
2. `[ledger]` What {settlement} can field and what {settlement} has built are both in good order; the town would take real effort to threaten and knows it.
3. `[street]` The town does not spend much time thinking about being attacked, and the reason is not complacency — the arrangements are in place, and the town is not relying on anything else instead.
4. `[counterforce]` Nothing presses {settlement} at present, and the absence is not luck: a town this well set is not worth the price of trying.

**readiness `ADEQUATE`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} is covered against what it is likely to face, with little left over — the arrangements work and would not survive being tested twice at once.
2. `[visitor]` A stranger sees an ordinary defended town at {settlement}: enough on the walls to be serious, not enough to be reassuring.
3. `[street]` The town believes it could hold against the usual trouble and does not claim more than that, which is a fair reading of what it has.
4. `[threshold]` {settlement} is at the point where the arrangements are sufficient and nothing is spare — one more demand on them and the town would be choosing which pressure to leave uncovered.

**readiness `WEAK`**
1. `[ledger]` The defensive arrangements at {settlement} are thin. Something is in place against most pressures and nothing is in place in depth.
2. `[visitor]` What a stranger notices at {settlement} is how much of the perimeter has nobody on it, and how normal the town seems to find that.
3. `[unfolding]` {settlement} is getting by on not being tested. The margin has been narrowing rather than widening, and nothing in hand is closing it.

**readiness `CRITICAL`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} is effectively undefended. There is no arrangement here that would slow a serious attempt on the town by more than the time it takes to walk in.
2. `[street]` The town knows perfectly well what it could not survive, and the knowledge shapes what it will and will not provoke.
3. `[visitor]` A stranger reaches the centre of {settlement} without being stopped, challenged or counted by anybody at all.

**terrain FAVOURABLE to the defender**
1. `[visitor]` The ground does more for {settlement} than the town does — the approach is narrow, and anything coming at it has to come the long way and in the open.
2. `[ledger]` {settlement} is sited where the terrain carries part of the defense; what the town has built is worth more here than the same works would be on flat ground.
3. `[counterforce]` The ground here does the arguing. Anything weighing an attempt on {settlement} has to weigh the approach first, and the approach is the part that does not improve with numbers.

**terrain EXPOSED**
1. `[visitor]` {settlement} sits open. There is no ground here that helps it, and everything the town has must be built rather than found.
2. `[ledger]` The site gives {settlement} nothing defensively — every advantage the town holds is one it has paid for and must keep paying for.
3. `[street]` The town has no hill and no narrows and does not pretend otherwise; what {settlement} holds it holds by standing on it.

**strategic value HIGH**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} holds something worth taking, and that is the whole of the defensive problem: the town must be worth more to keep than it is to seize.
2. `[street]` The town is aware of what it sits on and aware that others are aware, and the awareness runs underneath a great deal of what it decides.
3. `[visitor]` A stranger understands within a day why anybody would want {settlement}, and understands shortly afterwards why the town is so careful about who it offends.

**strategic value LOW**
1. `[counterforce]` Nothing at {settlement} is worth an army's season, and the town's best defense is that plain fact rather than anything on its walls.
2. `[ledger]` {settlement} is not a prize. What it can field matters less here than what it does not have that anyone would come for.
3. `[street]` The town knows it is not worth taking and has made a kind of peace with the knowledge, which is not the same as being comfortable with it.

---

### DS-DEF-2 — `Defense › Threat assessment (the five readiness rows)` · `defenseProfile.scores{monster,military,internal,economic,disaster} + institutions{walls,garrison,militia,charter} + config.monsterThreat + compound.inst`
> *the five pressures, and what the town actually has standing against each*

**RECEIPT:** `src/domain/display/threatAssessment.js:28-195` (`buildThreatAssessment`) · rendered `src/components/new/tabs/DefenseTab.jsx:150-183` · the walls predicate `src/domain/causalState.js:300-315` (`defenseProfileHasWalls`)
**STATE-KEY:** five fixed rows (`Beasts & Monsters` · `Invasion & War` ·
`Internal Security` · `Economic Survival` · `Disasters & Famine`), each with a
`scoreBand` badge (`STRONG` / `ADEQUATE` / `WEAK` / `CRITICAL`), read against
`config.monsterThreat` (`plagued` / `frontier` / `settled`), the institution
presence flags, and `compound.inst`.
**SLOTS:** `{settlement}` `{band}` `{route}`
**SECTION-TARGET:** `defense`
**PROVENANCE + FENCE (the biggest in this cluster).** The existing
`buildThreatAssessment` lattice is genuinely dossier-native and this shape
EXTENDS it into a pool rather than replacing it — the corpus's job here is that
each branch currently holds exactly ONE string, so every settlement in a given
branch says the same words. Two standing defects are corrected in the authoring
and must not be reintroduced: the `plagued`+nothing branch leaks a lowercase
sentence lead, and the walls read must ride `defenseProfileHasWalls`, never a
presence check on `institutions.walls` — an empty `walls: []` array still
contains the key, and reading presence rather than the predicate once granted
every settlement in the product the walled bonus. Institution presence is a
STANDING fact with no recorded history; the causal clauses here are
*capability* clauses (walls without people cannot be held) and never
*historical* ones (walls built after a siege) unless the history surface
supplies the ancestry.
**PDF PARITY:** parity (`viewModel.js` defense slice).

**`Beasts & Monsters` — `plagued`, perimeter AND organized force**
1. `[ledger]` The country around {settlement} is thick with creatures and the town has answered it properly: there is a wall to hold and there are people to hold it, and both are in use constantly.
2. `[street]` Defense at {settlement} is not an emergency arrangement, it is the week's work — the rotations run, the gates close on time, and nobody treats any of it as unusual.
3. `[unfolding]` What {settlement} has built is holding against the pressure and is being spent doing it; the posture is survivable, and survivable is the most that can be said of it here.

**`Beasts & Monsters` — `plagued`, perimeter but NO force to hold it**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} has a wall and nobody to man it. The line is a chokepoint on paper and a chokepoint requires people standing in it, which this town cannot supply for more than a night.
2. `[visitor]` A stranger walks the perimeter at {settlement} and finds long stretches of good work with nobody on them, in a country where that matters a great deal.
3. `[unfolding]` The works at {settlement} are doing less each season as the watch thins, and the thinning is not being reversed.

**`Beasts & Monsters` — `plagued`, NO perimeter and NO force**
1. `[ledger]` An embattled country and nothing organized standing in it: {settlement} has no line, no force and no specialist recourse, and survival here rests on terrain, distance and the ability to leave.
2. `[street]` The town does not defend itself. What it does is watch, and move, and hope the pressure goes around it, and that is understood by everyone in it.
3. `[visitor]` A stranger arriving at {settlement} understands the danger before anybody explains it, because nothing about the place is arranged as though danger were expected to be met.

**`Beasts & Monsters` — `frontier`, credible deterrence**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} sits on an active frontier with a line and a force behind it — most of what comes out of the country will not press a defended perimeter, and most of what comes here does not.
2. `[street]` The town takes the frontier seriously and has taken it seriously long enough that the arrangements are ordinary rather than anxious.
3. `[counterforce]` Very little reaches {settlement} out of the wild country, and the reason is that the arrangements are visible from a long way off.

**`Beasts & Monsters` — `frontier`, force without a perimeter**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} keeps armed people on an open frontier, which means the defense is reactive: whatever comes chooses where the fighting happens, and the town arrives afterwards.
2. `[visitor]` A stranger finds soldiers at {settlement} and no wall for them to stand on, and can see how that would go against anything that arrived in more than one place.
3. `[street]` The town can answer trouble and cannot prevent it, and the difference costs it something every season.

**`Beasts & Monsters` — `settled`, defenses beyond the need**
1. `[counterforce]` There is very little in the country around {settlement} and there are substantial works facing it; whatever the walls here are for, it is not the creatures.
2. `[ledger]` {settlement} is comfortably over-provided against beasts. The pressures that matter to this town are internal, and its defensive spending does not reflect that.
3. `[visitor]` A stranger notices the perimeter at {settlement} chiefly for how relaxed the people on it are.

**`Beasts & Monsters` — `settled`, nothing organized**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} keeps no organized defense against the country, and in a heartland this quiet the arrangement is a reasonable one rather than a gap.
2. `[street]` The town has never needed to think about what is outside it, and does not.
3. `[visitor]` A stranger walks out of {settlement} in any direction at any hour and meets nothing that would justify a watch.

**`Invasion & War` — walls AND professional garrison**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} has a line and professionals to hold it, which is real deterrence against raiding and against a conventional assault; it is not a posture rated for a long siege without stores behind it.
2. `[visitor]` A stranger sizing {settlement} up sees the two things that matter together — the wall and the men who belong to it — and revises what an attempt would cost.
3. `[street]` The town believes it could be held, and the belief is founded on something rather than on hope.

**`Invasion & War` — walls with citizen militia**
1. `[ledger]` Walls at {settlement} with townspeople behind them: credible against raiders, and inadequate against anybody who arrives professionally and brought siege gear.
2. `[street]` The town would turn out and does not pretend that turning out is the same as being defended.
3. `[unfolding]` What {settlement} has would hold against the first thing and is unlikely to hold against the second, and nothing in hand changes that.

**`Invasion & War` — walls with NO force**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} has walls and nobody to put on them. A determined attacker takes this town with ladders and patience, and requires nothing else.
2. `[visitor]` A stranger at {settlement} sees a serious perimeter and a serious absence of anyone standing in it.
3. `[street]` The town has the thing that would save it and not the people who would use it, and says so when pressed.

**`Invasion & War` — force with NO walls**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} keeps a professional force and no perimeter. It answers raiders well and cannot hold a siege, because there is nothing here to hold.
2. `[street]` The town's defense is people rather than works, and people can be gone around.
3. `[visitor]` A stranger sees soldiers at {settlement} and no line for them to stand behind, and can see how that decides where any fight would happen.

**`Invasion & War` — militia only**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} can put armed citizens on their own ground, which counts for something against a disorganized raid and for nothing at all against a disciplined force.
2. `[street]` The town knows its own country and knows that knowing it is not an answer to a professional army.
3. `[visitor]` A stranger at {settlement} meets armed townspeople who are entirely competent on their own ground and have never stood in a line with anybody.

**`Invasion & War` — neither walls nor force**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} has no line and no force. Organized aggression cannot be resisted here; what preserves the town is distance, diplomacy, or being beneath notice.
2. `[counterforce]` Nothing has come for {settlement} and nothing about the town would stop it — the safety here is entirely a matter of nobody having wanted to.
3. `[street]` The town's plan for an army is to not be interesting to one, and everybody here can state the plan.

**`Internal Security` — full legal chain (court AND prison)**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} can arrest, try and hold, and having all three means the town's law is a process rather than a threat.
2. `[street]` A thing done wrong at {settlement} goes somewhere and takes time, and the town has come to rely on that rather than on the watch's temper.
3. `[visitor]` A stranger who brings a complaint at {settlement} is given a procedure rather than a favour, and the procedure runs.

**`Internal Security` — court without detention**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} tries offences it cannot hold anyone for; the sentences available here are money and exile, and both of them fall unevenly.
2. `[street]` The town's law can name a wrong and cannot keep the person who did it, so it reaches for the purse or the road.
3. `[unfolding]` Each judgment {settlement} cannot enforce costs the next one a little of its weight, and the town's courts are spending down a reputation they cannot replace.

**`Internal Security` — detention without process**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} can hold people and has no settled way of deciding whether it should, which makes enforcement here a matter of who is doing it.
2. `[visitor]` A stranger at {settlement} is careful in a way he would not need to be in a town with courts, and cannot say precisely why.
3. `[street]` The town can put a person away at {settlement} and cannot say on what grounds, and has learned not to ask on whose.

**`Internal Security` — no legal infrastructure**
1. `[ledger]` There is no legal machinery at {settlement}; order here rests on force alone, and force alone deters only while it is present.
2. `[street]` The town settles things itself, quickly, and does not always settle them well.
3. `[visitor]` A stranger wronged at {settlement} discovers there is nowhere to take it, and that the discovery surprises nobody local.

**`Economic Survival` — `STRONG`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} can absorb a sustained crisis out of its own revenue: emergency measures can be paid for and the garrison can be kept paid while they last.
2. `[street]` The town could go through a bad season with its arrangements intact, and the people who would have to be paid through one know it.
3. `[counterforce]` Trouble at {settlement} has not turned into a collapse, and the reason is money — a town that can pay through a crisis mostly does.

**`Economic Survival` — `ADEQUATE`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} can fund a short crisis. A long one begins eating reserves within a few months, and the reserves are not deep enough to hide that from anybody.
2. `[unfolding]` The town's capacity to pay for its own emergencies is real and finite, and every season of pressure moves the finite part closer.
3. `[threshold]` {settlement} can pay for a crisis of the ordinary length; the edge of what it can fund lies a few months past the beginning of one, and the town has not been asked to find out where.

**`Economic Survival` — `WEAK`**
1. `[ledger]` Chronic shortfall at {settlement} limits what the town can do in an emergency before the emergency starts; the pay is irregular, and irregular pay shows up as morale exactly when it matters.
2. `[street]` The people who would have to hold {settlement} through something are already owed, and they have not forgotten it.
3. `[unfolding]` The shortfall at {settlement} is chronic rather than sudden, and each season of it removes a little more of what the town could do about a crisis when one comes.

**`Economic Survival` — `CRITICAL`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} cannot fund a response to anything. Any sustained pressure exhausts the town's capacity almost immediately and then continues.
2. `[unfolding]` The town is not spending its way out of trouble because there is nothing to spend, and each thing that goes wrong makes the next thing cheaper to happen.
3. `[street]` The town could not pay for a bad month at {settlement}, and the people who would have to be paid know it.

**`Disasters & Famine` — granary AND hospital**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} holds food against a bad year and has somewhere to put the sick; between them the town can take a failed harvest or an outbreak without either becoming a catastrophe.
2. `[street]` The town has a place for grain and a place for the ill, and knows exactly what having both is worth.
3. `[counterforce]` Neither a failed harvest nor an outbreak turns into a catastrophe at {settlement}, and the reason is in the two buildings rather than in the luck.

**`Disasters & Famine` — granary AND parish care only**
1. `[ledger]` There is food stored at {settlement} and there are clergy who tend the sick — reserves against hunger, and against disease something better than nothing and well short of a hospital.
2. `[street]` The town can eat through a bad year. What it does about a plague is pray and nurse, in that order.
3. `[visitor]` A stranger finds a full store and a modest infirmary at {settlement}, and can see which of the two the town has spent its thinking on.

**`Disasters & Famine` — granary, NO medical provision**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} can feed itself through a failed harvest and has nothing at all against disease; a sickness here spreads until it stops of its own accord.
2. `[unfolding]` The stores will carry the town through hunger. Nothing here will carry it through a plague, and the town has not built anything that would.
3. `[street]` The town can outlast a hungry year at {settlement} and has no answer at all to a sick one, and knows which of the two it fears.

**`Disasters & Famine` — NO reserves, hospital present**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} can treat and contain an outbreak and keeps no food against a bad harvest; a crop failure here becomes hardship the same season it happens.
2. `[street]` The town is better prepared for the sickness than for the hunger, which is an unusual way round and does not comfort anyone.
3. `[unfolding]` {settlement} is arranged against the sickness it has seen and not against the hunger it has not, and nothing in hand is correcting the imbalance.

**`Disasters & Famine` — NO reserves, NO medical provision**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} holds no food against a bad year and has nobody to treat the sick; a failed harvest is immediate hardship here and a plague runs until it burns out.
2. `[street]` The town's answer to a bad season is the same as its answer to a sickness, which is to endure it and count afterwards.
3. `[visitor]` A stranger looking for the granary or the sick-house at {settlement} is directed to neither, because there is neither.

---

### DS-DEF-3 — `Defense › Public order banner` · `defenseProfile.scores.internal + safetyProfile.{safetyLabel,safetyDesc}`
> *how safe the ordinary day is, said once*

**RECEIPT:** `src/components/new/tabs/DefenseTab.jsx:190-215` · classifier `src/domain/display/safetySeverity.js:47-73` · ladder `src/domain/compendium/bandLadders.js:186-195` (`SAFETY_LEVELS`)
**STATE-KEY:** `orderStatus` from `scores.internal` (`Strong` / `Adequate` /
`Weak Public Order`, plus `Critical: Order Failing`) · `safetyProfile.safetyLabel`
(`Very Safe` / `Safe` / `Moderate` / `Unsafe` / `Dangerous`) · `safetySeverityOf`
tier (`dangerous` / `unsafe` / `controlled` / `stable`, TOTAL over fourteen
producer tokens with a loud `unknown` fallback) · a crisis stress may OVERRIDE
the label with a compound form.
**SLOTS:** `{settlement}` `{band}` `{faction}`
**SECTION-TARGET:** `defense` · also `power`
**PROVENANCE + FENCE:** the surface currently says the same fact three times in
three grammars — a headline, a badge and a raw label — and the in-file comment
admits it. **The corpus says it once.** The variant IS the banner; the badge
becomes chrome and the raw label is not printed at all. Where a crisis stress
has produced a COMPOUND label, the prose must honour the compound and not the
base — a town reading *tense under an active siege* is not a `Moderate` town
and may not be described as one. The `unknown` fallback is LOUD by design and
draws no prose: an unclassifiable safety reading is a defect to surface, not a
state to narrate. A First-Survey page qualifies every cell here as a first
reading rather than a settled one.
**PDF PARITY:** parity.

**`Very Safe`**
1. `[street]` Nothing much happens to people at {settlement}. The watch is heavier than anything working against it, by a margin the town does not have to think about.
2. `[visitor]` A stranger walks {settlement} at any hour without being troubled, and notices that the locals do the same without appearing to have decided to.
3. `[ledger]` Enforcement at {settlement} sits far above whatever criminal presence the town holds; the returns are thin because there is very little to return.
4. `[counterforce]` There is money at {settlement} and there is opportunity, and neither has produced an organized interest — the watch here is simply too heavy to be worth the arrangement.

**`Safe`**
1. `[street]` {settlement} is a quiet town. Things are taken occasionally and the taking does not spread, because the people who would spread it are known.
2. `[ledger]` Enforcement at {settlement} is clearly the stronger side; criminal work here is suppressed rather than absent, and stays suppressed.
3. `[visitor]` A stranger is comfortable at {settlement} and is also aware that the comfort has somebody maintaining it.

**`Moderate`**
1. `[street]` {settlement} runs on a working balance: the law is present, crime exists, and neither has the run of the town.
2. `[ledger]` Enforcement and criminal presence at {settlement} sit close enough that the town's condition is a matter of which way the next few seasons go.
3. `[visitor]` A stranger at {settlement} is told which streets to avoid and finds the advice specific, short and accurate.
4. `[threshold]` The balance at {settlement} sits where either side could take it — a thinner watch or a bolder operator would show up in the returns within the season, and nothing else would need to change.

**`Unsafe`**
1. `[street]` Crime at {settlement} is outrunning the watch. It is not chaos; it is that the people doing it have stopped expecting to be caught.
2. `[ledger]` What criminal work is being done at {settlement} measurably exceeds what is being answered, and the gap is legible in the returns rather than merely felt.
3. `[unfolding]` The balance at {settlement} has tipped and is still tipping — each season the watch answers a smaller share of what happens.

**`Dangerous`**
1. `[street]` The watch at {settlement} has been overwhelmed. What happens in this town is decided by whoever is present when it happens.
2. `[ledger]` Organized interests at {settlement} now outweigh enforcement outright; there is no part of the town's order that does not run through somebody's permission.
3. `[visitor]` A stranger at {settlement} is not warned about particular streets, because the warning would have to cover all of them.

**COMPOUND override (a crisis stress has rewritten the label)**
1. `[street]` The ordinary safety of {settlement} has stopped applying. Whatever the town was, the crisis on it now is what governs the streets.
2. `[ledger]` The town's usual reading does not describe {settlement} at present; the standing condition has been overtaken by the crisis and the watch is arranged around that instead.
3. `[unfolding]` Order at {settlement} is being held under a pressure the town's arrangements were not built for, and how long it holds is a question about the pressure rather than about the watch.

**First-Survey qualification (the reading is a first look)**
1. `[visitor]` This is what {settlement} looks like on a first pass; a season here would either confirm it or correct it, and nobody has spent the season yet.
2. `[ledger]` The safety reading for {settlement} is an initial one. It is honest about what has been seen and says nothing about what has not.
3. `[street]` What the town says about its own safety at {settlement} is what the town says on a first acquaintance, and a longer one would be a different account.

---

### DS-DEF-4 — `Defense › Criminal structure + capture consequence` · `deriveCriminalStructure(settlement) + powerStructure.criminalCaptureState + safetyProfile.criminalInstitutions[]`
> *what shape the crime here has, and how far into the hall it has reached*

**RECEIPT:** `src/domain/display/defenseDisplay.js:97-180` (the three structure notes `:139-150`) · `src/components/new/tabs/DefenseTab.jsx:217-256` (the two capture-consequence paragraphs `:249-255`) · ladder `src/domain/corruption.js:421-441`
**STATE-KEY:** structure key (`organized` · `semi-organized` · `diffuse` ·
`null`) classified by institution NAME substring · `criminalCaptureState`
(`none` · `adversarial` · `equilibrium` · `corrupted` · `capture`, ordered) ·
`criminalInstitutions[]` with per-operation roles (`parallel marketplace` ·
`duty evasion` · `unlicensed revenue` · `money laundering` · `stolen goods
market` · `protection + extraction` · `criminal revenue stream`).
**SLOTS:** `{settlement}` `{seat}` `{faction}` `{npc}` `{good}` `{band}`
**SECTION-TARGET:** `defense` · also `power` (the capture arm)
**PROVENANCE + FENCE (R-W2-A).** The structure key is a substring classification
over institution names — a variant may assert only what its matched class
means, and a `null` key means *no organized criminal infrastructure was
recognized*, which is NOT the same as *no crime*. Capture is a separate ladder
and never inferred from structure: an `organized` town at `none` capture is
common and means exactly that the syndicate has not reached the seat. Capture
at `corrupted` and above is covert content — the town does not know, and the
page that says so is the DM's. Never name the compromised person as a target;
the leash is the state, not a fate.
**PDF PARITY:** parity for structure; `PRINT-DEFERRED` for capture rungs at
`corrupted` and above (the print chapter carries no audience gate of its own).

**structure `organized`**
1. `[street]` Crime at {settlement} has an order to it. What is permitted is permitted by somebody, random violence is put down because it draws attention, and the arrangement is stable enough to negotiate with.
2. `[ledger]` The criminal interest at {settlement} is a hierarchy rather than a nuisance: predictable, rule-bound, and extracting steadily from the same places every season.
3. `[visitor]` A stranger at {settlement} is not robbed and is quietly told what the arrangement is; the danger here is systematic rather than sudden.
4. `[counterforce]` There is very little street violence at {settlement} and it is not the watch's doing — a structured interest suppresses what draws enforcement, and does it more thoroughly than the watch could.

**structure `semi-organized`**
1. `[ledger]` Criminal work at {settlement} is coordinated enough to hold routes and territories and not coordinated enough to have a head; several interests are running, and none of them is the one to talk to.
2. `[street]` The town knows there are arrangements at {settlement} and cannot say whose, because the answer changes by street and by season.
3. `[unfolding]` What is being contested at {settlement} is who gets to be the arrangement, and until that settles the town gets the cost of both a syndicate and a scramble.

**structure `diffuse`**
1. `[ledger]` Crime at {settlement} is opportunistic and unorganized — fences, bandits and small operators working for themselves, with nothing to threaten and nobody to buy.
2. `[street]` Things go missing at {settlement} and no one profits by it twice; there is no structure here, which makes it less dangerous and much harder to stop.
3. `[visitor]` A stranger at {settlement} is at risk from individuals rather than from an arrangement, which is worse in the moment and better in every other way.

**structure `null` (nothing organized recognized)**
1. `[ledger]` No criminal infrastructure has been identified at {settlement}. Whatever wrongdoing the town has does not run through anything that could be called an organization.
2. `[street]` What goes wrong at {settlement} goes wrong one thing at a time and belongs to whoever did it.
3. `[visitor]` A stranger asking after the arrangements at {settlement} is met with genuine incomprehension, which is either the truth or an unusually good performance of it.

**capture `none`**
1. `[ledger]` Nothing criminal has reached the {seat} at {settlement}. Whatever operates in the town operates outside the hall, and the hall's decisions are the hall's.
2. `[counterforce]` There is a criminal interest at {settlement} with the means to reach into the hall and it has not; the seat's people are watched too closely for the approach to be worth its risk.
3. `[street]` Whatever is being run at {settlement} is being run outside the hall, and the town's dealings with the hall are the town's dealings with the hall.

**capture `adversarial`**
1. `[ledger]` The {seat} at {settlement} and the criminal interest here are opposed, actively, and the opposition is the arrangement — each is spending on the other and neither is winning.
2. `[street]` The hall at {settlement} moves against the operators and the operators move back, and the town is where the two of them meet.
3. `[unfolding]` The hall and the operators at {settlement} are each spending on the other and neither is gaining, and the spending is coming out of things the town needed.

**capture `equilibrium`**
1. `[ledger]` The {seat} at {settlement} and the criminal interest have found a line and both keep to it; nothing is bought here, and nothing is pressed either.
2. `[street]` There is an understanding at {settlement} that nobody has written down: what is permitted, and where the permitting stops.
3. `[unfolding]` The balance at {settlement} holds because both sides prefer it, which is a different thing from either side being restrained.

**capture `corrupted`** *(every variant `dm-only`)*
1. `[ledger · dm-only]` Somebody at the {seat} of {settlement} is carrying a leash, and the decisions that come out of the hall now bend around it in ways the rolls will not show.
2. `[unfolding · dm-only]` The criminal interest at {settlement} has bought into the hall rather than around it, and each decision it shapes makes the next one cheaper to shape.
3. `[street · dm-only]` The town at {settlement} does not know, and is beginning to notice that certain matters never quite conclude.

**capture `capture`** *(every variant `dm-only`)*
1. `[ledger · dm-only]` The {seat} at {settlement} is held. What the hall decides is decided elsewhere and announced here, and the announcing is the only part the town sees.
2. `[unfolding · dm-only]` There is no longer a distinction at {settlement} between the criminal interest and the governing one; the town is being administered by its syndicate through the forms of its hall.
3. `[visitor · dm-only]` A stranger at {settlement} conducts perfectly ordinary business with the hall and never learns whose business it actually was.

### DS-DEF-5 — `Defense › Armed forces & fortifications` · `defenseProfile.institutions{walls,garrison,militia,watch,mercenary,charter,magicDef}`
> *what is actually standing here, and what is not*

**RECEIPT:** `src/components/new/tabs/DefenseTab.jsx:284-328` (the two authored absence sentences `:305-307`, `:324-326`)
**STATE-KEY:** presence and content of each institution group —
`Fortifications` (walls) · `Standing Forces` (garrison, militia, watch) ·
`Contracted Forces` (mercenary) · `Monster Response` (charter) · `Arcane
Defense` (magicDef).
**SLOTS:** `{settlement}` `{faction}` `{band}` `{counterpart}`
**SECTION-TARGET:** `defense`
**PROVENANCE + FENCE (R-W2-B).** The surface today narrates only ABSENCE and
lists presence as name-and-description cards. This shape authors presence at
the same density. A group's presence asserts capability, never history: a
garrison exists, it was not necessarily raised for anything. Walls ride
`defenseProfileHasWalls`, never a presence check. Contracted forces carry one
entailment the standing forces do not — they are paid, and the state carries
the payment relationship, so a variant may say the contract is a contract; it
may not say the contract is failing unless the economic arm supplies that.
**PDF PARITY:** parity.

**walls PRESENT**
1. `[visitor]` There is a line around {settlement} and it is kept — the gates shut, the works are repaired, and a stranger enters where the town intends him to.
2. `[ledger]` {settlement} controls its own entry points. That is what a perimeter buys: not invulnerability, but the choice of where anything happens.
3. `[street]` The town has a wall and arranges its day around the gates without noticing that it does.

**walls ABSENT**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} is unfortified. There is no perimeter, which means the town cannot control entry and cannot make a chokepoint of anything.
2. `[visitor]` A stranger arrives at {settlement} from whichever direction suits him and is not obliged to pass anybody on the way in.
3. `[street]` The town has an edge rather than a boundary at {settlement}, and the edge is wherever the last building happens to be.

**garrison PRESENT**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} keeps a standing force — people whose work is the defense of this town and who are answerable for it as work.
2. `[street]` The soldiers at {settlement} belong to the town rather than to a season; they are a fixture, with all the friction and all the reliability of one.
3. `[visitor]` A stranger at {settlement} is counted at the gate by somebody whose job that is, and is counted again on the way out.

**militia PRESENT (no garrison)**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement}'s defense is its own people under arms. They know the ground, they can be raised, and raising them stops everything else the town was doing.
2. `[street]` The town turns out when it must and goes back to work after, and understands that this is a different thing from having soldiers.
3. `[counterforce]` {settlement} could keep soldiers and does not; the town has decided that arming itself when needed costs less than paying anybody to be armed all year.

**watch PRESENT**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} keeps a watch, which is a matter of order rather than of war — it answers what happens inside the walls and is not built for what arrives outside them.
2. `[street]` There are people walking the town at {settlement} at night, and the town's sense of itself rests more on that than on anything at the gate.
3. `[visitor]` A stranger walking {settlement} late is looked at rather than stopped, and the looking is systematic enough to notice.

**NO organized force at all**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} has no organized force. Defense here is individual armed citizens, with no command, no training and no way to coordinate a response.
2. `[street]` The town would defend itself as a crowd defends itself, which is to say briefly and in several directions at once.
3. `[visitor]` A stranger at {settlement} can find nobody whose responsibility the town's defense is, because it is not anybody's.

**mercenary / contracted forces PRESENT**
1. `[ledger]` Part of what defends {settlement} is contracted rather than owed — competent, available, and loyal for exactly as long as the terms run.
2. `[street]` The town pays for some of its soldiers and knows which ones, and treats them accordingly.
3. `[unfolding]` The contracted companies at {settlement} are doing the work they were engaged for, and the engagement has an end written into it that the town has not yet had to think about.

**charter hall PRESENT (specialist monster response)**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} keeps a charter for specialist work — the things a garrison is wrong for go to people retained to handle exactly them.
2. `[street]` There is somewhere at {settlement} to take the problems that are not soldiers' problems, and the town uses it more than it likes to say.
3. `[visitor]` A stranger at {settlement} finds a hall whose business is the sort of trouble a garrison is the wrong instrument for, and finds it busy.

**charter hall ABSENT where the country warrants one**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} retains no specialists. Whatever comes out of the country is the garrison's problem regardless of whether the garrison is the right answer to it.
2. `[counterforce]` {settlement} keeps no chartered hall and is not unaware of the need; what stands between the town and one is the cost of retaining specialists rather than any doubt about wanting them.
3. `[street]` The town sends soldiers to things soldiers are wrong for at {settlement}, and pays for the wrongness each time.

**arcane defense PRESENT**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} keeps arcane provision in its defenses: detection, wards, and an answer to things that conventional arrangements cannot see coming.
2. `[visitor]` A stranger at {settlement} is measured at the gate in a way he cannot quite identify, and is not told about it.
3. `[street]` There are wards at {settlement} the town does not discuss and would notice immediately if they stopped.

**arcane defense ABSENT**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement}'s defense is conventional throughout. What arrives unseen here goes undetected and therefore unanswered.
2. `[street]` The town has no answer to anything it cannot see, and is unprepared rather than protected in that respect.
3. `[counterforce]` Nothing unseen has come at {settlement}, and nothing here would know if it had; the record of safety and the capacity to detect are two different things.

### DS-DEF-6 — `Defense › Supporting capabilities` · `scores.economic/magical + compound.inst{hasMagicInst,hasCourtSystem,hasPrison,hasHospital,hasChurch,hasGranary,hasPort,hasNavy} + tradeAccess + blockade`
> *the six things that decide whether the defense can be sustained rather than merely fielded*

**RECEIPT:** `src/domain/display/defenseDisplay.js:183-250` (`deriveSupportingCapabilities`) · rendered `src/components/new/tabs/DefenseTab.jsx:330-357`
**STATE-KEY:** six rows — `Economic Backing` (`Well-funded` / `Adequate` /
`Underfunded` / `Critical`) · `Magical Capability` (`Arcane support` / `None`) ·
`Legal Infrastructure` (`Court + Prison` / `Court only` / `Prison only` /
`None`) · `Medical Readiness` (`Hospital present` / `Clergy care` / `None`) ·
`Logistics & Supply` (`Granary present` / `No reserves`, modulated by port and
isolation) · `Naval Defense` when coastal (`Under blockade` / `Naval force` /
`Port only`).
**SLOTS:** `{settlement}` `{route}` `{counterpart}` `{institution}` `{band}`
**SECTION-TARGET:** `defense` · also `economy` (the backing and logistics arms)
**PROVENANCE + FENCE:** this is the corpus's closest existing model for state+cause
weaving and the causal clauses here are all CAPABILITY entailments — a granary
plus sea access is the hardest siege posture to break because of what those two
things do together, not because of anything that happened. The one genuinely
historical cell is the blockade: `stressor-blockade:` is a stamped cause with a
recorded author, and it is the only row here that may name a counterpart. The
magical bypass channels (`teleport` / `airship`) are live-world state.
**PDF PARITY:** parity, EXCEPT the blockade bypass cells — `PRINT-DEFERRED`.

**`Economic Backing: Well-funded`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} pays its defense in full and on time, keeps the equipment maintained and holds something in reserve; every other arrangement in the town's defense is worth more for it.
2. `[street]` The soldiers at {settlement} are paid, which the town regards as unremarkable and which is the single thing most likely to keep them where they are.
3. `[counterforce]` A bad year does not empty {settlement}'s walls, and the reason is the pay rather than the loyalty — a town that keeps paying keeps its people standing where it put them.

**`Economic Backing: Adequate`**
1. `[ledger]` Upkeep at {settlement} is adequate with shortfalls; things are maintained a little later than they should be and nothing has yet failed for it.
2. `[unfolding]` The defense at {settlement} is being funded to the edge of sufficiency each season, and a single unbudgeted year would show immediately.
3. `[threshold]` Upkeep at {settlement} covers what is standing and nothing more; the edge is one unbudgeted expense away, and nothing in the arrangement is holding the town back from it.

**`Economic Backing: Underfunded`**
1. `[ledger]` Pay at {settlement} is irregular and the equipment shows it; the risk here is not capability but morale, and morale fails before equipment does.
2. `[street]` The people who defend {settlement} are owed and are saying so, and the saying has begun to change how they do the work.
3. `[unfolding]` The gear at {settlement} is being kept in service past where it should be replaced, and each season of that makes the replacing more expensive rather than less.

**`Economic Backing: Critical`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} cannot sustain armed forces at all. This is not a shortfall, it is a systemic failure — whatever is still standing is standing for reasons other than payment.
2. `[unfolding]` The town has stopped being able to pay for its own defense and has not yet stopped having one, and the gap between those two facts is closing.
3. `[street]` Nobody at {settlement} is being paid to defend it, and the people still doing it are doing it for reasons the town cannot rely on.

**`Magical Capability: Arcane support`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} has arcane provision behind its defenses — detection, warding and the ability to answer a spell with something other than a shield.
2. `[street]` The town has people it calls on for the things soldiers cannot do, and calls on them rarely enough that it still counts as remarkable.
3. `[counterforce]` What the walls at {settlement} cannot see is somebody's charge rather than nobody's, and the town would learn of such a thing from an account rendered rather than from an alarm raised.

**`Magical Capability: None`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement}'s defense is entirely conventional. Anything that arrives invisible arrives unopposed, because there is nothing here that would know.
2. `[counterforce]` Nothing has exploited the gap at {settlement}, and the gap is complete — whatever has spared the town, it was not a ward.
3. `[street]` The town's answer to anything it cannot see at {settlement} is to find out afterwards, and nothing in its arrangements offers it any other answer.

**`Legal Infrastructure: Court + Prison`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} holds the whole chain: arrest, prosecution and detention. A wrong here has a route through the town's institutions and the route ends somewhere.
2. `[street]` Justice at {settlement} takes time and produces a result, and the town has arranged its expectations around both halves of that.
3. `[visitor]` A stranger at {settlement} sees both buildings on the same square and understands what the town has decided about order.

**`Legal Infrastructure: Court only`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} tries and cannot hold. The sentences available are fines and exile, and both of them favour whoever can pay or leave.
2. `[unfolding]` The gap at {settlement} between what a court can decide and what the town can carry out has widened, and it widens a little more each time somebody walks away from a ruling.
3. `[street]` The town at {settlement} fines what it cannot hold and exiles what it cannot fine, and both fall hardest on people who have nowhere to go.

**`Legal Infrastructure: Prison only`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} can detain without any settled process for deciding whom; enforcement here is arbitrary in the exact sense, and depends on who is holding the keys.
2. `[street]` People are taken at {settlement} and the taking is not explained, and the town has learned not to press for the explanation.
3. `[unfolding]` What {settlement} holds people for drifts with whoever is doing the holding, and the drift is not written anywhere it could be corrected.

**`Legal Infrastructure: None`**
1. `[ledger]` There is no legal machinery at {settlement}. Deterrence extends exactly as far as force does and stops there.
2. `[visitor]` A stranger wronged at {settlement} has nowhere to take it, and is told so without embarrassment.
3. `[street]` Order at {settlement} extends exactly as far as somebody willing to enforce it is standing, and no further.

**`Medical Readiness: Hospital present`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} can treat casualties, contain an outbreak and recover people who would otherwise be lost; in a siege that is the difference between losses and attrition.
2. `[street]` There is a place at {settlement} for the badly hurt, and the town's willingness to fight is quietly founded on it.
3. `[visitor]` A stranger taken ill at {settlement} is taken somewhere rather than left somewhere, which is rarer than a town this size suggests.

**`Medical Readiness: Clergy care`**
1. `[ledger]` Care at {settlement} is the parish's: wounds dressed, sickness nursed, and no capacity at all for containment when something spreads.
2. `[street]` The town's sick go to the {institution} and are tended there, well and without means.
3. `[unfolding]` What {settlement} has will hold against the ordinary run of hurt and would be overwhelmed by anything arriving all at once.

**`Medical Readiness: None`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} has no dedicated healers. Wounds take what they take, and a plague here runs until it has finished.
2. `[unfolding]` The town is one outbreak from a loss it has nothing prepared to slow.
3. `[street]` The sick at {settlement} are tended by whoever is related to them, and that is the whole of the arrangement.

**`Logistics & Supply: Granary + port`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} holds stored food and keeps the sea; between them that is the hardest posture there is to starve out, and it is the reason a siege here would have to be a fleet.
2. `[counterforce]` {settlement} does not close easily — cutting the roads leaves the water, closing the water leaves the stores, and an attempt on either one alone accomplishes nothing.
3. `[street]` The town has grain in store and water at the door at {settlement}, and is unhurried about threats in a way that irritates its neighbours.

**`Logistics & Supply: Granary in isolation`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} holds reserves and nothing else; endurance here is exactly the size of what is in the granary and not a day longer.
2. `[unfolding]` The town can sit out a bad season on its stores, and the stores are a quantity that only goes one way once the sitting starts.
3. `[street]` What is in the store at {settlement} is the whole of the town's future in a bad season, and everybody here can estimate it.

**`Logistics & Supply: Granary with road supply`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} keeps stores and is fed by the roads; the roads are the vulnerability, and the granary is what buys time to do something about them.
2. `[street]` The town has grain put by and knows it is put by against the {route} closing rather than against the harvest failing.
3. `[counterforce]` An interruption on the roads to {settlement} does not reach the table, and the granary is the reason rather than the roads.

**`Logistics & Supply: No reserves, port open`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} keeps nothing back and does not need to while the port runs; the supply continues as long as the water does.
2. `[counterforce]` {settlement} keeps no granary and is not short for want of one — the water answers faster than a store could, and a store is not what this town would spend on.
3. `[street]` The town eats what the water brings at {settlement}, daily, and has no cause to wonder what it would do otherwise.

**`Logistics & Supply: No reserves, landlocked`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} holds no food buffer at all. Any interruption to supply becomes a survival question within days rather than seasons.
2. `[street]` The town eats what arrives, and has nothing put by against a season when nothing does.
3. `[unfolding]` {settlement} is one interrupted week from a genuine crisis and has been for as long as anybody has bothered to check.

**`Naval Defense: Naval force`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} controls its own sea approaches; anything coming by water would need to beat the fleet before it reached the town.
2. `[visitor]` A stranger arriving by sea at {settlement} is met well before the harbour and understands the arrangement immediately.
3. `[counterforce]` Nothing hostile stands off {settlement}, and the ships that would have to be beaten first are the reason.

**`Naval Defense: Port only`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} has a harbour and nothing to hold it with; any vessel that wants the sea approaches has them.
2. `[unfolding]` The port is the town's living and the town's opening, and nothing has yet arrived that made the second half matter.
3. `[visitor]` A stranger sails into {settlement} unchallenged and is struck, afterwards, by how easy that was.

**`Naval Defense: Under blockade`** *(recorded cause — clause licensed)*
1. `[ledger]` A hostile fleet sits across {settlement}'s sea approaches. The port is choked, and what the town lives on has stopped arriving by the route it arrives on.
2. `[street]` The harbour at {settlement} is full of vessels that cannot leave and empty of the ones that should be coming in.
3. `[unfolding]` The blockade on {settlement} is doing its work quietly — nothing has been assaulted, and everything the town needs is a little further away each week. *(print-deferred)*
4. `[counterforce · dm-only]` The fleet across {settlement}'s approaches has closed the water and not the town: a magical channel still runs the line, impaired and expensive and running. *(print-deferred)*

### DS-DEF-7 — `Defense › Live defense readiness + war front` · `causalState.variables.defense_readiness{band,contributors[]} vs frozen defenseProfile.scores · warStatus.{besiegedBy,besiegingTargets}`
> *what the town can field TODAY, against what it was built to field*

**RECEIPT:** `src/components/dossier/EngineSections.jsx:143-198` (the two siege sentences `:178-195`) · the verbatim twin `src/components/new/tabs/WarFaithTab.jsx:73-76`
**STATE-KEY:** `defense_readiness.band` (`surplus` · `adequate` · `strained` ·
`critical` · `collapsed`) · `contributors[]{delta, reason}` (each carries a
RECORDED reason) · `warStatus.besiegedBy[]` · `warStatus.besiegingTargets[]`.
**SLOTS:** `{settlement}` `{counterpart}` `{reason}` `{band}` `{timeband_since}`
**SECTION-TARGET:** `defense` · also `tensions`
**PROVENANCE + FENCE:** this is the ONE defense shape whose state carries real
provenance — every contributor has a `reason`, so causal clauses are fully
licensed and are the point. The live band must be read AGAINST the frozen
profile: the interesting sentence is nearly always the divergence (a
well-built town currently unable to field it). Per **R-W2-C** the siege
sentence is minted HERE and drawn by `WarFaithTab` rather than authored twice;
the verbatim duplication between `EngineSections.jsx` and `WarFaithTab.jsx` is
a live copy-drift hazard and the corpus must not preserve it. `besiegingTargets`
is the campaigning arm and says nothing about the home town's walls except
through the thinning it causes, which IS recorded.
**PDF PARITY:** `PRINT-DEFERRED` for the contributor cells (the print chapter
self-gates the live world); parity for the band cells.

**band `surplus`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} can field more than it needs. Whatever the town was built for, what it can actually put in the line today exceeds it.
2. `[street]` There are more people under arms at {settlement} than the season calls for, and the surplus is doing nothing except being available.
3. `[counterforce]` Nothing threatens {settlement} and the town is at full strength anyway, which is a choice somebody made and is still paying for.

**band `adequate`**
1. `[ledger]` What {settlement} can field today matches what the town needs today, with the margin that implies and no more.
2. `[street]` The arrangements are holding at {settlement} and nobody is being asked for anything extraordinary.
3. `[counterforce]` Nothing has had to be set aside at {settlement} to keep the line manned, which is the only real test of whether a defense is affordable.

**band `strained`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} can still field a defense and it is costing the town elsewhere; what is standing in the line is standing because something else was set down.
2. `[unfolding]` The strain at {settlement} is being carried rather than relieved, and carrying it is what is producing the next season's shortfall.
3. `[street]` The town is managing, and everybody involved in the managing knows precisely how thin it is.
4. `[threshold]` {settlement} is at the point where holding the line and running the town have become the same budget, and each further call on one is taken from the other.

**band `critical`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} is at the edge of being able to field anything. What remains is committed, and there is nothing behind it.
2. `[unfolding]` One more call on {settlement}'s defense and there will be nothing to answer it with; the town has reached the point of hoping rather than planning.
3. `[street]` What is left at {settlement} is committed, and the town has stopped discussing what it would do if more were asked.

**band `collapsed`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} can no longer field a defense. The arrangements exist on the rolls and do not exist in the town.
2. `[street]` There is nobody left at {settlement} to be called out, and the calling has stopped.
3. `[visitor]` A stranger at {settlement} finds the posts unmanned and the muster books current, and the gap between the two is the whole of the town's condition.

**live band FALLS SHORT of the built profile**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} was built to be defended better than it presently can be; the works are what they were and the people to hold them are not.
2. `[visitor]` A stranger sees serious defenses at {settlement} and very few defenders, and is right to read the gap as recent.
3. `[unfolding]` The distance between what {settlement} has and what {settlement} can use is widening, and the works do not narrow it by standing there.

**live band EXCEEDS the built profile**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} is fielding more than its works would suggest — what is holding this town is people rather than stone, and it is holding.
2. `[street]` The town has put more into the line than it was ever set up to, and the arrangement is temporary in a way nobody has named yet.
3. `[counterforce]` {settlement} is holding more than its works should permit, and what is holding it is people under no obligation to be there.

**a contributor with a RECORDED reason, adverse**
1. `[ledger]` What {settlement} has lost from its readiness it has lost to {reason}, and the loss is entered rather than inferred.
2. `[unfolding]` {reason} is still taking from what {settlement} can field, season on season, and nothing in hand has been set against it.
3. `[street]` The town can name what has cost it at {settlement}, exactly, and names it whenever the subject of the walls comes up.

**a contributor with a RECORDED reason, favourable**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement}'s readiness stands where it does because of {reason}; take that away and the town's line is a different arrangement entirely.
2. `[street]` The town is better defended than it was, and can name the thing that did it.
3. `[counterforce]` {settlement} would be thinner on the walls without {reason}, and the difference is entered rather than assumed.

**`besiegedBy` present** *(the shared siege row — minted once, per R-W2-C)*
1. `[street]` There is an army at {settlement}'s walls. Everything the town does now is done inside them, and the gates have stopped being a matter of routine.
2. `[ledger]` {counterpart} holds the approaches to {settlement}. Nothing enters and nothing leaves, and the town's condition from here is a question of stores and time.
3. `[visitor]` Nobody reaches {settlement} at present. What can be seen of the town is seen from outside a line that is not the town's own.

**`besiegingTargets` present (the army is abroad)**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement}'s force is away besieging {counterpart}, and the home garrison is thinned for exactly as long as the campaign runs.
2. `[street]` The town has sent its soldiers to sit outside somebody else's walls, and is quieter and emptier and more exposed for it.
3. `[unfolding]` What {settlement} is spending on {counterpart} it is spending out of its own walls; the campaign is being paid for in the town's own readiness.

### DS-DEF-8 — `Defense › Active military status` · `settlement.stress[].type → DEFENSE_STRESS_STATUS + stress.{summary,viabilityNote}`
> *when a crisis overrides the standing posture*

**RECEIPT:** `src/components/new/tabs/DefenseTab.jsx:138-148` · the set `src/domain/display/defenseDisplay.js:29, :61` (`DEFENSE_STRESS_STATUS`, derived over `STRESS_TYPE_MAP`)
**STATE-KEY:** the matched stress `type` against `DEFENSE_STRESS_STATUS`
(fifteen postures, total over the registered stress vocabulary) ·
`stress.summary` · `stress.viabilityNote`.
**SLOTS:** `{settlement}` `{counterpart}` `{band}` `{reason}`
**SECTION-TARGET:** `defense`
**SIBLING:** `DS-STR-1` / `DS-GEN-4` frame what a stressor does to the TOWN; this block and `DS-DEF-10` frame what it does to the town's ability to DEFEND itself. A composed page draws one, not both.
**PROVENANCE + FENCE:** the posture words are UPPERCASE military-telegram chrome
and are NOT the prose — the corpus supplies the sentence and the posture word
remains a badge if the surface wants one. A stress carries a TYPE, which is
its own cause, so each posture licenses exactly the clause its type names and
no other. The fifteen per-posture cells live in **`DS-DEF-10`**; this shape authors
only the OVERRIDE framing — the fact that the town's standing defensive
description has been superseded — plus the `summary` / `viabilityNote` weave.
Shared with the PDF `defenseSlice`.
**PDF PARITY:** parity (pdf-4 shares the set).

**override active (generic framing)**
1. `[ledger]` What {settlement}'s defenses are ordinarily worth does not describe the town at present; there is a crisis on it, and the arrangements have been rebuilt around the crisis.
2. `[street]` The town's usual posture has been set aside at {settlement}. Everything is arranged for the one thing now, and the rest is waiting.
3. `[unfolding]` {settlement} is not being defended according to its plan; it is being defended according to what is happening, and the two have parted company.

**override active, viability threatened**
1. `[unfolding]` The crisis at {settlement} is not merely a posture — it is a question about whether the town continues, and the defensive arrangements are being spent to answer it.
2. `[ledger]` What is standing at {settlement} is standing against something the town was not built to survive, and the reckoning says so plainly.
3. `[street]` The town is not arguing about its defenses at {settlement}; it is arguing about whether there will be a town to defend.

**override active, viability intact**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} is under real pressure and is not in danger of ending; the posture has changed and the town has not.
2. `[street]` The town is dealing with something serious and going on being a town while it does.
3. `[counterforce]` The crisis at {settlement} has changed the posture and not the prospects, and the town has been careful to keep the two separate.

**multiple stresses, one posture shown**
1. `[ledger]` More than one thing is pressing {settlement} at once, and the posture named is the heaviest of them rather than the whole account.
2. `[unfolding]` The town is carrying several pressures together at {settlement}, and it is the combination rather than any single one that is doing the damage.
3. `[street]` More than one thing is wrong at {settlement} at once, and the town names the loudest of them when asked and means all of them.

### DS-DEF-9 — `Viability › Magic dependency` · `defenseProfile.magicDependency + economicState.activeChains[].magicNote`
> *whether the town would still work if the practitioners left*

**RECEIPT:** `src/components/new/tabs/ViabilityTab.jsx:171-191` · twin badge `src/components/new/tabs/OverviewTab.jsx:188-194`
**STATE-KEY:** `magicDependency` boolean · `activeChains[].magicNote` (which
chains name a magical input).
**SLOTS:** `{settlement}` `{good}` `{band}` `{institution}`
**SECTION-TARGET:** `viability` · also `overview`
**PROVENANCE + FENCE:** this is currently ONE fixed paragraph shown to every
settlement in the product that trips the boolean — the corpus's job here is
variance and the FALSE state, which today has no prose at all. The clause is
conditional and consequential (*losing them would expose…*), which is lawful:
the dependency is a standing structural fact and the exposure is entailed by it,
not by any event. The named causes of a hypothetical loss must stay generic —
conflict, sickness, political disruption — because the state carries no
specific threat to the practitioners. Twinned with the OverviewTab badge per
**R-W2-C**: one pool, two draws.
**PDF PARITY:** parity (`viewModel.js` viability slice).

**`magicDependency` true**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} runs on arcane work. What the town produces, and in some measure what the town eats, depends on practitioners continuing to be here and continuing to be willing.
2. `[street]` A handful of people at {settlement} do work nobody else can do, and the town's whole arrangement rests on them without ever having decided to.
3. `[unfolding]` The resilience of {settlement} is only as durable as its practitioners are; a hard season for them — conflict, sickness, a falling-out with the hall — becomes a hard season for everyone at once.
4. `[visitor]` A stranger at {settlement} sees a town doing better than its ground and its roads would explain, and eventually finds out why.

**`magicDependency` true, with a NAMED dependent chain**
1. `[ledger]` The {good} that {settlement} lives on cannot be made here without arcane work, which puts the whole line behind a very small number of people.
2. `[unfolding]` {settlement}'s trade in {good} is being sustained by practitioners rather than by the country, and nothing about that arrangement is being made more robust.
3. `[street]` The town's living at {settlement} passes through a workshop that could not run without the arcane, and everybody in the trade knows which workshop.

**`magicDependency` false**
1. `[ledger]` Nothing structural at {settlement} depends on arcane work. What the town makes it makes with hands and ground, and would go on making without a practitioner in it.
2. `[counterforce]` There is magic at {settlement} and the town does not rest on it — losing every practitioner here would be a loss and not a collapse.
3. `[street]` The town would go on if the arcane went away, more slowly and less comfortably and go on.
