### DS-DEF-10 — `DEFENSE — the five arms, readiness badges, and military posture` · `defenseProfile.{scores,economicGates,institutions} + defenseProfileHasWalls + DEFENSE_STRESS_STATUS`
> *the frozen ladder cells, and the fifteen crises that override them*

**RECEIPT:** `src/domain/display/defenseScoreBands.js:29-40` (`scoreBand`, the one ladder) · `src/domain/compendium/bandLadders.js:197-208, :239-240` (`DEFENSE_READINESS_LEVELS`, the five arms) · `src/domain/display/defenseDisplay.js:26-68` (`MILITARY_POSTURE`, `DEFENSE_STRESS_STATUS`) · `src/domain/causalState.js:300-315`
**STATE-KEY:** `scoreBand` (four, FROZEN — *never extend*: `STRONG` ≥65 ·
`ADEQUATE` ≥40 · `WEAK` ≥20 · `CRITICAL` <20) applied per arm · the five arms
(beasts and monsters · invasion and war · internal security · economic survival
· disasters and famine) · the settlement's overall defense reading from *well
defended* down to *undefended* · `DEFENSE_STRESS_STATUS`, fifteen postures, one
per registered stress type.
**SLOTS:** `{settlement}` `{counterpart}` `{band}` `{reason}` `{good}` `{timeband_since}`
**SECTION-TARGET:** `defense`
**SIBLING:** `DS-GEN-3` reads the five score axes at OVERVIEW grain as timeless first-survey capability prose under WRITER-4's carve-out; the badge cells here are the DEFENSE-tab reading and carry no such carve-out. `DS-STR-1` owns the stressor's effect on the town; the postures here own its effect on the defense.
**PROVENANCE + FENCE (the walls hazard, restated because it has bitten).** Walls
ride `defenseProfileHasWalls(def)` — the `hasWalls` flag, or a genuinely
non-empty `walls` / `institutions.walls`. The read was once a stringify match
that granted every settlement in the product the walled bonus, because an empty
`walls: []` array still contains the literal key. **No variant may assert walls
from a presence check.** The badge ladder is frozen at four rungs and prose that
implies a fifth gradation is prose that will not survive the next tuning. The
per-arm row framing lives in **`DS-DEF-2`**; this shape authors the badge cells
generically (any arm) and the fifteen postures, which no rendered surface
currently narrates at all beyond an uppercase word.
**COMPOSITION FENCE (§0h V1-g):** the `ADEQUATE` badge cell here and
`DS-DEF-1`'s `ADEQUATE` readiness cell read closely alike and describe different
rows — the arm and the town. Both stand; **one per page, never both.**
**PDF PARITY:** parity (pdf-4 shares `DEFENSE_STRESS_STATUS`).

**badge `STRONG` (any arm)**
1. `[ledger]` This pressure is well covered at {settlement}. What the town has against it is more than the pressure is likely to bring.
2. `[street]` Nobody at {settlement} worries about this one, and the not-worrying is founded on something rather than on habit.
3. `[counterforce]` Nothing gets far with this at {settlement}. What the town holds against it caps how bad it can become, and the cap sits well above anything this pressure brings.

**badge `ADEQUATE` (any arm)**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} is covered against this and has very little margin; the arrangements work and would not survive being tested twice.
2. `[street]` The town would manage, and knows roughly how narrowly.
3. `[threshold]` What {settlement} has here answers the pressure at its ordinary size; the edge is an unusual one, and nothing about the arrangement would announce that the edge had been reached.

**badge `WEAK` (any arm)**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} is thinly covered here. Something is in place, and a real instance of this would strain it past where it holds.
2. `[unfolding]` What {settlement} keeps against this arm has been falling behind what the arm is asked to meet, and no decision taken here has changed that direction.
3. `[threshold]` The arrangement at {settlement} holds against the small instance of this and not the large one, and there is nothing in it that distinguishes the two until the large one arrives.

**badge `CRITICAL` (any arm)**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} is effectively uncovered against this. Whatever the town has here would not slow the thing it is meant to slow.
2. `[street]` The town knows this is where it would break and has not been able to do anything about it.
3. `[visitor]` A stranger can see the gap at {settlement} without being told what to look for, and can see that the town has stopped looking at it.

**overall reading `well defended`**
1. `[visitor]` {settlement} is a defended town in every direction a stranger would think to look, and in the two he would not.
2. `[ledger]` The five pressures on {settlement} are all answered, none of them thinly; there is no obvious way in and no obvious way to starve it out.
3. `[counterforce]` Nothing presses {settlement}, and the arrangements are the reason for that rather than the beneficiary of it.

**overall reading `undefended`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} has no serious answer to any of the five pressures on it. This is not a weak point, it is the absence of the whole arrangement.
2. `[street]` The town's continued existence is a matter of nothing having come for it.
3. `[visitor]` A stranger enters {settlement}, crosses it and leaves without meeting anything that would have stopped him doing worse.

**posture `ACTIVE SIEGE`** *(recorded cause — clause licensed)*
1. `[ledger]` An army sits outside {settlement} and the town's defensive arrangements have been reorganized around holding rather than deterring.
2. `[street]` Everything the town does now is measured against the stores and the walls, and nothing else is being measured at all.
3. `[unfolding]` Every arrangement at {settlement} is now measured against the stores, and the measuring is the town's whole calendar.

**posture `INTERNAL PRESSURE` (famine)**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} is defending a hungry town, which changes what can be asked of anyone in it and how long they will keep answering.
2. `[street]` The watch stands and the watch is not being fed properly, and both of those facts are the same fact.
3. `[unfolding]` What {settlement} can ask of the people on its walls falls with what it can feed them, and neither is being replenished.

**posture `UNDER OCCUPATION`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement}'s defense is not {settlement}'s. What stands on these walls answers to {counterpart}, and what the town could raise for itself is a separate and much smaller question.
2. `[street]` The soldiers here defend the town from everything except the people who put them here.
3. `[counterforce]` {settlement} is well garrisoned and cannot use the garrison; what stands here defends the town against everything except the arrangement that put it here.

**posture `COMMAND SPLIT` (politically fractured)**
1. `[ledger]` There is more than one authority at {settlement} that believes it commands the town's defense, and the arrangements are being duplicated and contradicted in equal measure.
2. `[unfolding]` Nothing at {settlement} has failed yet because of the split, and everything is taking longer, and the taking-longer is what will fail first.
3. `[street]` Orders reach the gate at {settlement} from two directions, and the people at the gate have quietly decided which to obey.

**posture `UNDER TRIBUTE` (indebted)**
1. `[ledger]` What {settlement} would spend on its own defense goes elsewhere first; the tribute is met and the garrison is met afterwards, out of what remains.
2. `[street]` The town pays before it protects, and everybody who has to stand a watch here understands the order.
3. `[unfolding]` The tribute at {settlement} is met first and the walls are met with the remainder, and the remainder is smaller each year.

**posture `SECURITY COMPROMISED` (recently betrayed)**
1. `[ledger]` Something at {settlement} was given away recently, and the town's defensive arrangements are being reworked on the assumption that what was given away is known.
2. `[street · dm-only]` The watch at {settlement} has changed its rounds and will not say why, and the town can work out why.
3. `[unfolding]` Everything {settlement} knew about its own arrangements must now be assumed to be known elsewhere, and re-arranging all of it is slower than the town has.

**posture `INFILTRATION ACTIVE`** *(every variant `dm-only`)*
1. `[ledger · dm-only]` There is somebody inside {settlement}'s arrangements who does not belong to them, and the defense is being conducted around a hole nobody has located.
2. `[unfolding · dm-only]` Each measure {settlement} takes is a measure the other side learns about, and the town has not yet stopped taking them.
3. `[street · dm-only]` The watch at {settlement} has begun keeping things from itself, which is the only defense available against a hole nobody has found.

**posture `QUARANTINE ACTIVE` (plague onset)**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} is closed against a sickness rather than against an army; the gates are held, and they are held against the town's own country.
2. `[street]` Nobody comes in and nobody goes out, and the reason is not war, which the town finds harder rather than easier.
3. `[unfolding]` The gates at {settlement} are being held against the town's own country, and every week of that costs the town something the country would have supplied.

**posture `SUCCESSION CONTESTED`**
1. `[ledger]` There is no settled authority to order {settlement}'s defense, and the arrangements are being kept by people acting on what the last authority wanted.
2. `[unfolding]` The town's defense is running on momentum at {settlement}, and momentum has a length to it that nobody has measured.
3. `[street]` Nobody at {settlement} can order the walls held and several people could, and the several are watching each other rather than the country.

**posture `BEAST PRESSURE`**
1. `[ledger]` What is coming out of the country at {settlement} is coming steadily, and the town's arrangements are being spent on it faster than they are being replaced.
2. `[street]` The watch here is a matter of what is outside rather than who is inside, and it does not stand down.
3. `[unfolding]` The pressure at {settlement} does not pause between attempts, and what the town spends answering it is not being replaced at the same rate.

**posture `INSURGENCY ACTIVE`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} is being defended against people who live in it, which is a different problem from a siege and one the town's arrangements were not built for.
2. `[unfolding]` Every measure taken against the insurgency at {settlement} makes the next one more necessary, and the cycle is the shape of the problem rather than an accident of it.
3. `[street]` The watch at {settlement} is walking streets that do not welcome it, and that is more dangerous to it than any ambush.

**posture `RELIGIOUS UPHEAVAL`**
1. `[ledger]` The town's defense at {settlement} has been caught in a question about observance; what is being contested is who may command, and the answer turns on creed.
2. `[street]` The people who would stand together on the walls at {settlement} are not currently speaking, and the reason is not military.
3. `[unfolding]` Who may give an order at {settlement} has become a question about observance, and until it is answered the answer to every other question is delayed.

**posture `REVOLT ACTIVE`**
1. `[ledger]` A part of {settlement}'s own population has risen, and the town's arrangements are being used inward rather than outward.
2. `[unfolding]` What is happening at {settlement} will end, and how it ends will decide what the town is afterwards rather than merely who holds it.
3. `[street]` The town's arms at {settlement} are pointed inward, and everybody holding one knows at whom.

**posture `WAR FOOTING` (wartime)**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} is arranged for war throughout — what is produced, what is stored, and who is available are all being decided against it.
2. `[street]` The town has stopped doing several ordinary things and has stopped remarking on having stopped.
3. `[unfolding]` What {settlement} produces, stores and spares is all being decided against the war, and the deciding has reached things the town did not expect it to reach.

**posture `MIGRATION SURGE`**
1. `[ledger]` More people are arriving at {settlement} than the town's arrangements were built for, and the defense of the place has become a question of order rather than of walls.
2. `[street]` The town is fuller than it was by {band}, and nothing about the watch, the water or the gates has been enlarged to match.
3. `[visitor]` A stranger arriving at {settlement} is not remarkable, because so many people have arrived lately that the gate has stopped treating arrival as an event.

### DS-WAR-1 — `War & Faith › War block` · `settlementWarStatus + settlementWarExhaustion + settlementMobilization + settlementOccupation + occupierHoldings`
> *the town's war, from the walls outward*

**RECEIPT:** `src/components/new/tabs/WarFaithTab.jsx:40-107` (the `Line` component's bold-lead shape) · phrases `src/domain/display/occupationStatus.js:38-71`, `src/domain/display/mobilizationStatus.js:27-47, :124-131`, `src/domain/display/warStatus.js:360-366`
**STATE-KEY:** `statusLabel` (`Under siege` · `On campaign` · `Occupied` · `At
war`) · `warExhaustion` band (`rested` · `near peace` · `war-weary` ·
`exhausted`) · `mobilization{phrase, ticksToDeploy, covert}` ·
`occupation{occupierName, statePhrase, resistancePhrase, pays, burdened}` ·
`occupierHoldings{holds[], stretchedThin, strengthened}` · the six
`FEASIBILITY_PHRASE` verdicts.
**SLOTS:** `{settlement}` `{counterpart}` `{band}` `{timeband_since}` `{timeband_span}` `{reason}`
**SECTION-TARGET:** `tensions` · also `defense`
**PROVENANCE + FENCE (the register's sharpest conflict, R-W2-D).** The current
surface prints a bolded lead label and then the clause — *Under siege.* then the
sentence — which is the Herald's headline-and-telling shape rendered inside the
town page, and the woven test forbids it outright. Every variant below carries
its own state in one sentence so the lead has nothing left to do. Underneath the
label, the read-model phrases themselves are already dossier-native and are
**extended, not replaced**. Two leaks are corrected and may not return: the raw
tick count (*roughly three ticks from marching*) becomes a time band, and the
`besiegedBy` sentence is drawn from **`DS-DEF-7`**'s pool rather than authored a
second time here (R-W2-C). Siege truth rides `isLiveWarFront` — a bare hostile
relationship mints a `confirmed` front with no army behind it, and the phantom
front is never narrated as a siege. `settlementWarStatus` returning null is
PEACE, not missing data.
**PDF PARITY:** parity (`liveWorld.js` carries the same selector phrases).

**`statusLabel: Under siege`** *(draws `DS-DEF-7`'s siege pool; no second authoring)*

**`statusLabel: On campaign`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement}'s army is in the field against {counterpart}, and everything the town does at home is done with that absence in it.
2. `[street]` The town is short of the people who would ordinarily be here, and the shortness is felt at the harvest and the gate before it is felt anywhere else.
3. `[unfolding]` What {settlement} wins from this campaign it will win somewhere else; what it is spending, it is spending here, continuously and in plain view.
4. `[visitor]` A stranger finds {settlement} quieter than a town this size should be, and finds the reason is arithmetic rather than mood.

**`statusLabel: Occupied`**
1. `[street]` {settlement} is held by {counterpart}. The hall still sits, the market still opens, and neither of them decides anything that {counterpart} has an opinion about.
2. `[ledger]` What is decided at {settlement} is decided subject to the occupier; the town's institutions run, and run inside a boundary somebody else drew.
3. `[visitor]` A stranger at {settlement} deals with the town's own people and notices, eventually, whose soldiers are standing where the town's would be.

**`statusLabel: At war` (no front at the walls, no army abroad)**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} is at war with {counterpart} and nothing has yet come of it here — no army has marched and none has arrived, and the town is arranged for both.
2. `[street]` The town is at war in the way a town can be at war without seeing any: prices, absences, and a great many conversations about what might happen.
3. `[counterforce]` {settlement} is at war with {counterpart} and has not been touched by it; the distance between the two towns is doing the work that walls would otherwise have to.

**`warExhaustion: rested`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} carries no scar from fighting. Whatever the town has done, it has recovered from, and what it could put in the field it could put there tomorrow.
2. `[street]` The town has its people and its temper both, and neither has been spent on anything recently.
3. `[counterforce]` {settlement} could put its full strength in the field this season and is not being asked to; nothing has been spent, and the not-spending is a choice somebody made.

**`warExhaustion: near peace`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} carries a light mark from its fighting and is very nearly clear of it; what remains is a preference rather than a weakness.
2. `[unfolding]` The town is close to being done with the war it fought, and the last of it is going out of the place slowly.
3. `[threshold]` {settlement} is at the edge of being clear of its war; another quiet season closes it, and a single hard one would put the town back where it was.
4. `[street]` The town talks about the fighting in the past tense now, with the occasional slip.

**`warExhaustion: war-weary`**
1. `[street]` {settlement} has had enough. The fighting has gone on past what the town believed it agreed to, and the agreement is being withdrawn quietly in a hundred small refusals.
2. `[ledger]` Sustained fighting has worn {settlement} down to the point where the hall's appetite and the town's have parted; pressure to end it is now internal.
3. `[unfolding]` The town is being pushed toward suing for peace by its own weariness rather than by any defeat, and the pushing gets harder to resist each season.
4. `[threshold]` {settlement} has reached the point where the next demand is the one that gets refused, and nobody in the hall can say in advance which demand that will be.

**`warExhaustion: exhausted`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} is spent. There is no further war in this town — not unwillingness, absence — and anything asked of it from here will not arrive.
2. `[street]` The town has nothing left to give the war and has stopped pretending it might find some.
3. `[unfolding]` Whatever {settlement} agrees to next it will agree to because it is exhausted, and the terms will show it.

**mobilization — climbing the ramp, still distant**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} is turning toward war and is not near it; the muster has begun and the economy has barely started shifting behind it.
2. `[street]` The town has begun to prepare in the way towns prepare — quietly, and well ahead of anybody admitting what for.
3. `[visitor]` A stranger at {settlement} notices the town buying things it does not ordinarily buy, and is told nothing about why.

**mobilization — climbing, close to ready**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} is within a season of being able to march. What remains is the last of the provisioning, and the provisioning is nearly done.
2. `[unfolding]` The town is very close to war-ready now, and nothing about the preparation looks reversible from inside it.
3. `[street]` The town has stopped pretending at {settlement}; the preparations are visible, and the visibility is now part of the point.

**mobilization — fully ready**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} is mobilized. The town could march this season, and everything it is doing instead is a choice.
2. `[street]` The soldiers are ready at {settlement} and are being kept waiting, and being kept waiting is its own kind of pressure.
3. `[unfolding]` {settlement} is holding a war it has already paid for, and every season it holds it the paying continues without anything being gained.

**mobilization — COVERT** *(every variant `dm-only`)* · **COMPOSITION FENCE (§0h V1-g):** the muster read; `DS-WAR-5`'s covert-*posture* cell is the disposition read. **One per page, never both.**
1. `[ledger · dm-only]` {settlement} is preparing for war without saying so. The muster is being taken as something else, and the accounts have been arranged to agree.
2. `[street · dm-only]` Nothing at {settlement} looks like a town going to war, which at this stage is the intended appearance.
3. `[counterforce · dm-only]` {settlement} would be visibly arming by now if it intended to be seen, and the absence of any sign is the strongest sign there is.

**`occupation.pays` false (the occupation is a net drain on the holder)**
1. `[ledger]` Holding {settlement} is costing {counterpart} more than {settlement} yields; the garrison here is an expense the occupier has not yet found a way to end.
2. `[unfolding]` {counterpart} is bleeding into {settlement} rather than out of it, and every season of that makes the holding harder to justify at home.
3. `[street]` The soldiers holding {settlement} are costing whoever sent them more than the town has ever produced, and the town is aware of the arithmetic.

**`occupation.pays` true**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} has begun to pay {counterpart} back for taking it; the town's yield now exceeds what the garrison costs, and the arrangement has become worth keeping.
2. `[street]` What the town produces leaves it, and the leaving has become regular enough that the town budgets around it.
3. `[unfolding]` What leaves {settlement} for {counterpart} has become regular enough to be planned around at both ends, which is what makes an occupation permanent.

**`occupierHoldings.stretchedThin`**
1. `[ledger]` {counterpart} holds more than it can garrison properly, and {settlement} is one of the places where the thinness shows.
2. `[counterforce]` The occupier at {settlement} does not press harder because it cannot — what it holds elsewhere is taking the strength that pressing here would need.
3. `[street]` The garrison at {settlement} is smaller than it was and has not been reinforced, and the town has drawn the obvious conclusion about how {counterpart} is doing elsewhere.

**`occupierHoldings.strengthened`**
1. `[ledger]` What {counterpart} holds elsewhere is feeding what it holds here; the garrison at {settlement} is stronger for the occupier's other successes rather than in spite of them.
2. `[unfolding]` The occupier's grip on {settlement} is tightening because the occupier is winning elsewhere, and nothing local is causing it or can undo it.
3. `[counterforce]` Nothing {settlement} has done has loosened {counterpart}'s hold, because the hold is not being decided here.

**feasibility `plausible`**
1. `[ledger]` An attempt on {settlement} by {counterpart} would be a real contest with the outcome genuinely open, which is the most dangerous position a town can be in.
2. `[street]` The town believes it could be taken and believes it could hold, and lives with both beliefs at once.
3. `[unfolding]` Neither {settlement} nor {counterpart} can be confident of the outcome, which is precisely the condition under which somebody eventually tries.

**feasibility `auto_fail`**
1. `[counterforce]` {counterpart} cannot take {settlement}. The disparity is not close, and everybody on both sides has done the arithmetic.
2. `[ledger]` An assault on {settlement} by {counterpart} would be hopeless, which is why no assault comes and why the hostility persists in other forms.
3. `[street]` The town does not lose sleep over {counterpart}, and the not-losing-sleep is well founded.

**feasibility `harassment`**
1. `[counterforce]` {counterpart} is too weak to take {settlement} and strong enough to make the approaches unpleasant, which is precisely what it does.
2. `[ledger]` What {settlement} faces from {counterpart} is raiding rather than war; the town itself is not at risk and the country around it is.
3. `[street]` The outlying country around {settlement} is dangerous because of {counterpart} and the town itself is not, and the distinction matters a great deal to the people living outside the walls.

**feasibility `require_coalition`**
1. `[counterforce]` {counterpart} could not threaten {settlement} alone and knows it; whether this town is in danger is a question about who else {counterpart} can bring.
2. `[ledger]` {settlement} is safe from {counterpart} singly and not from {counterpart} in company, and the difference is the whole of the town's diplomacy.
3. `[unfolding]` What {settlement} watches is not {counterpart}'s army but {counterpart}'s company, and the company has been growing.

**feasibility `require_betrayal`**
1. `[counterforce · dm-only]` {settlement} cannot be taken from outside by {counterpart}; it would have to fracture first, and the fracturing is what {counterpart} is actually working on.
2. `[ledger]` The danger to {settlement} from {counterpart} is internal by necessity — the walls are not the way in, and both parties understand which way is.
3. `[street · dm-only]` {counterpart} has stopped looking at {settlement}'s walls and started looking at its quarrels, and the change of attention is the whole threat.

**feasibility `require_magic`**
1. `[counterforce]` Nothing conventional {counterpart} could bring would take {settlement}; only decisive arcane force would open this town, and that is a different kind of undertaking entirely.
2. `[ledger]` {settlement}'s defense against {counterpart} holds against everything except the arcane, which narrows the threat considerably and does not remove it.
3. `[street]` The town's confidence at {settlement} stops precisely where the arcane begins, and it has no comfortable way of discussing that.

### DS-WAR-2 — `War & Faith › Treaty block` · `renderTreatiesForSettlement(worldState,sid) → docs[]{complianceState, termLines[]{family,type,yearsRemaining,fraying,strainLine}}`
> *the peace as it is actually being kept, term by term*

**RECEIPT:** `src/domain/display/treatyDocument.js:35-91` (`TREATY_COMPLIANCE_VOICE` `:35-73`, `TREATY_COMPLIANCE_FLOOR` `:75-79`) · rendered `src/components/new/tabs/WarFaithTab.jsx:112-140` · families `src/domain/worldPulse/peaceTerms.js:164-200` (`TERM_CATALOG`)
**STATE-KEY:** seven term families (`economic` · `relational` · `security` ·
`territorial` · `political` · `informational` · `sovereignty`) × three
compliance states (`honored` · `strained` · `defaulted`), plus the generic
floor for an unnamed family · `yearsRemaining` · `fraying` · document-level
`complianceState` · which side of the treaty this page is (`victorId` /
`loserId`).
**SLOTS:** `{settlement}` `{counterpart}` `{term}` `{good}` `{band}` `{timeband_span}` `{timeband_since}`
**SECTION-TARGET:** `relations` · also `tensions`
**PROVENANCE + FENCE:** `TREATY_COMPLIANCE_VOICE` is the **model corpus for this
whole program** — twenty-one authored, present-tense, causal, in-world
sentences, already indexed family × band — and this shape is pool DEPTH on top
of it, not a replacement. Every existing string stays. The one leak corrected:
the UI prints a raw `· 4y left`, and duration here speaks only through the time
bands. A term's family names what the term IS, which is the recorded cause the
clause rides on; a term does not carry why the war was fought and no variant
asserts it. `stream: true` terms execute an installment each live tick, and
that is exactly what licenses the *still being paid* construction the owner's
granary exemplar turns on — a non-streaming term may not use it. Arms matter:
the same term reads differently from the payer's page and the receiver's, and
each cell below is authored from the page-owner's side.

**`economic` · `honored`**
1. `[ledger]` The tribute out of {settlement} goes out on time and in full; the wagons are loaded on the day, and the clerks who load them have stopped remarking on it.
2. `[street]` What the town owes {counterpart} it pays, and pays without incident, and the paying has become one of the ordinary burdens of the year.
3. `[elder]` The obligation is {timeband_span} old at {settlement} and has never once been missed, which is a kind of achievement nobody here wants credit for.

**`economic` · `strained`**
1. `[ledger]` The tribute out of {settlement} is paid and paid grudgingly — the stores complain, the wagons run late, and every season somebody proposes that this be the last one.
2. `[street]` The granary stands lighter than it should at {settlement}, and the lightness is the bill of the war, still being settled.
3. `[unfolding]` What {settlement} sends {counterpart} is still going and is going more slowly each season, and the slowing is being noticed at the other end.

**`economic` · `defaulted`**
1. `[ledger]` The wagons out of {settlement} have stopped. What was promised to {counterpart} is no longer being sent, and the oath that promised it is broken here.
2. `[street]` The town has stopped paying and has not said so formally, which everyone involved understands to be the same as saying so.
3. `[unfolding]` {settlement} has defaulted on what it owed {counterpart}, and what happens next is entirely {counterpart}'s decision.

**`relational` · `honored`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} answers {counterpart}'s muster as the terms require, without enthusiasm and without delay; the banner goes because it must and it goes on time.
2. `[street]` The town sends its people when it is called and does not pretend to be glad about it, and the not-pretending is permitted.
3. `[visitor]` A stranger at {settlement} watches the muster go out to a war the town has no interest in, on time and in silence.

**`relational` · `strained`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement}'s banner answers {counterpart} slowly now, and the ranks that answer it talk about not answering next time where they can be overheard.
2. `[unfolding]` The compelled alliance is holding at {settlement} by less each season, and the muster that arrives is smaller and later than the one before it.
3. `[street]` Fewer go each time from {settlement}, and fewer of those who go come back to the same houses.

**`relational` · `defaulted`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} has turned away from {counterpart}'s muster. The forced allyship is renounced, and the renunciation was public.
2. `[street]` The town will not send its people to {counterpart} again, and has said so where it cannot be taken back.
3. `[unfolding]` {settlement} has refused the muster and the refusal cannot be walked back; what happens next is {counterpart}'s to decide.

**`security` · `honored`**
1. `[ledger]` The cap on arms holds at {settlement}; no muster gathers where the terms forbid one, and the town has arranged its defense around what remains permitted.
2. `[counterforce]` {settlement} could raise more than it does and does not; the terms with {counterpart} are being kept where nobody is watching, which is the only place keeping them means anything.
3. `[street]` The town keeps within what it agreed at {settlement}, including in the seasons when keeping to it was plainly against its interest.

**`security` · `strained`**
1. `[ledger]` The cap on arms at {settlement} is fraying — drills are held quietly and just inside the letter of what was agreed, and the letter is being read generously.
2. `[street]` The town trains without calling it training, and everybody involved knows exactly which word is doing the work.
3. `[counterforce]` The muster at {settlement} has not gathered and everything short of gathering has been done, which is the term being tested rather than broken.

**`security` · `defaulted`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} has thrown off the cap on arms; the forbidden muster gathers in the open and has stopped being disguised as anything else.
2. `[unfolding]` The town is arming past what it agreed to and is not hiding it, which means the next move belongs to {counterpart}.
3. `[street]` The town is arming at {settlement} in daylight, and has stopped offering any account of it.

**`territorial` · `honored`**
1. `[ledger]` {counterpart}'s garrison keeps the walls of {settlement} without incident — resented thoroughly, and unremoved.
2. `[street]` The soldiers who hold the town are not the town's and there is no trouble about it, which is not the same as there being no feeling about it.
3. `[unfolding]` The garrison at {settlement} keeps the walls without incident and has kept them long enough that the absence of incident is beginning to look like the arrangement rather than the truce.

**`territorial` · `strained`**
1. `[street]` The garrison keeps {settlement}'s walls uneasily now; the townspeople chafe, stones are thrown, and the throwing has stopped being the work of children.
2. `[unfolding]` What was a quiet occupation at {settlement} is becoming a contested one, incident by incident, and no single incident has been large enough to name.
3. `[ledger]` The garrison at {settlement} is spending more of its strength on the town than on the walls, and the proportion is moving the wrong way.

**`territorial` · `defaulted`**
1. `[ledger]` The garrison at {settlement} is besieged inside the very town it holds; the occupation is repudiated and the repudiation is armed.
2. `[street]` The town has turned on the soldiers holding it, and the soldiers are now holding a position rather than a place.
3. `[unfolding]` What holds {settlement}'s walls is now under attack from inside them, and the occupation has become a siege in reverse.

**`political` · `honored`**
1. `[ledger]` The installed seat at {settlement} still sits, propped exactly as precariously as it was on the day it was set, and propped effectively.
2. `[visitor]` A stranger at {settlement} deals with a hall that plainly answers elsewhere, and finds it functions perfectly well on that basis.
3. `[street]` The town at {settlement} deals with the installed seat because it must, and deals with it competently, which is not the same as accepting it.

**`political` · `strained`**
1. `[ledger]` The installed seat at {settlement} wavers; its writ runs thinly beyond the hall itself, and the parts of the town that ignore it are growing.
2. `[unfolding]` What was set up at {settlement} to govern is governing less each season, and nothing has been sent to shore it up.
3. `[street]` The installed seat at {settlement} is obeyed inside the hall and increasingly nowhere else, and the boundary moves inward each season.

**`political` · `defaulted`**
1. `[ledger]` The installed seat at {settlement} is cast down and the strings that held it are cut; whatever governs here now, it is not {counterpart}'s arrangement.
2. `[street]` The town took its hall back, and the taking was not gentle and was not disputed by anybody who lives here.
3. `[unfolding]` The seat {counterpart} set at {settlement} is gone, and what stands in its place has not yet had to say what it will do about the rest of the terms.

**`informational` · `honored`**
1. `[ledger]` The court at {settlement} stays open to {counterpart}'s watching eyes exactly as the clause requires, and the watchers see what they were promised.
2. `[street]` There are people at the hall who are not the town's and everybody knows which, and the arrangement is tolerated as the price of the peace.
3. `[visitor]` A stranger at {settlement}'s hall finds people present who are plainly not the town's and are plainly entitled to be there.

**`informational` · `strained`**
1. `[ledger]` The openness of {settlement}'s court is narrowing; the observers are seeing less than they are owed, and are not yet in a position to insist.
2. `[unfolding]` Doors at {settlement} that were meant to stay open are closing one at a time, and none of the closings alone is worth an incident.
3. `[street]` The observers at {settlement} are still in the room and are being told less in it, and neither side has said so aloud.

**`informational` · `defaulted`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement}'s court has closed its doors. The disclosure clause is dead, and {counterpart}'s eyes are outside the building.
2. `[street]` The observers have been put out of the hall at {settlement}, politely and without any pretence about what it means.
3. `[unfolding]` The hall at {settlement} has closed and {counterpart} now knows only what it can find out, which is a different relationship entirely.

**`sovereignty` · `honored`**
1. `[ledger]` No foreign banner marches on {counterpart}'s succession and none marches on {settlement}'s; the pledge of non-intervention holds on both sides of it.
2. `[counterforce]` There has been a contest at {counterpart} that {settlement} could have reached into and did not, and the restraint is entered as such.
3. `[street]` Neither town interferes in the other's quarrels, and at {settlement} that restraint is discussed as a point of pride rather than of policy.

**`sovereignty` · `strained`**
1. `[ledger · dm-only]` Foreign coin stirs beneath {settlement}'s internal contests again, and the pledge of non-intervention frays at the edges where the coin lands.
2. `[unfolding · dm-only]` Somebody outside is beginning to have opinions about who governs {settlement}, and the opinions are arriving with money attached.
3. `[street · dm-only]` Somebody at {settlement} is being funded from outside, and the funding is careful enough that nothing can yet be said about it in the hall.

**`sovereignty` · `defaulted`**
1. `[ledger]` An army has crossed into {settlement}'s internal quarrel; the pledge of non-intervention lies broken, and openly.
2. `[street]` The town's own dispute is no longer the town's own, and the people who made it somebody else's are visible from the gate.
3. `[unfolding]` {settlement}'s internal quarrel now has a foreign army in it, and whatever it was about before, it is about that now.

**generic floor · `honored`** *(unnamed family)*
1. `[ledger]` The term is kept at {settlement} as it was written, and the peace holds along this seam of it.
2. `[street]` Whatever was agreed is being done, and the town has stopped watching to see whether it will be.
3. `[visitor]` A stranger at {settlement} would not know from the town's conduct that there was a term here at all, which is what keeping one looks like.

**generic floor · `strained`**
1. `[ledger]` The term is kept at {settlement} only barely; the strain of keeping it shows, and the showing is getting worse rather than better.
2. `[unfolding]` What was agreed is still being done at {settlement}, later and more grudgingly each season, and nobody has yet decided to stop.
3. `[street]` The town keeps what it agreed at {settlement} and has begun to talk openly about the cost of keeping it.

**generic floor · `defaulted`**
1. `[ledger]` The term is broken at {settlement}, and this seam of the peace has torn.
2. `[street]` What was agreed is not being done, and the town has stopped explaining why.
3. `[unfolding]` This part of the agreement has failed at {settlement}, and the parts still standing are now carrying the weight of it.

**`fraying` set on a term with time still to run**
1. `[unfolding]` This part of the peace at {settlement} has {timeband_span} left on it and will not reach the end of it in the shape it started.
2. `[ledger]` The term still has its years to run and is failing inside them, which is a different problem from a term expiring.
3. `[street]` Everybody at {settlement} expects this term to fail before it expires, and expects it openly enough that the expecting is helping it along.

**document-level: the treaty runs out within the year**
1. `[ledger]` What binds {settlement} to {counterpart} expires within the year, and both towns have started arranging for what follows rather than for what remains.
2. `[street]` The peace has a short time left in it, and the town has begun to talk about afterwards as a real place.
3. `[threshold]` The peace at {settlement} has a short time left in it, and what replaces it is a decision nobody has made, which is itself the most dangerous fact about the coming season.

**document-level: the town is the LOSER side**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} signed this peace from beneath and the terms show it throughout; nothing in the document was negotiated so much as accepted.
2. `[elder]` The town has carried these terms {timeband_since} and has stopped describing them as recent.
3. `[street]` The town at {settlement} calls the document by its proper name and has never once called it an agreement.

**document-level: the town is the VICTOR side**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} wrote these terms and {counterpart} keeps them, and what the town is owed arrives because the town won rather than because it asked.
2. `[unfolding]` What {settlement} took at the table is still arriving, and the arriving is what the victory actually consists of now.

### DS-WAR-3 — `War & Faith › Whole-tab dormant note` · `no war beat AND no treaty AND faith HIDDEN`
> *two whole subsystems being quiet, in one sentence*

**RECEIPT:** `src/components/new/tabs/WarFaithTab.jsx:216-220`
**STATE-KEY:** `settlementWarStatus` null · no treaty document for this
settlement · faith panel in HIDDEN mode.
**SLOTS:** `{settlement}`
**SECTION-TARGET:** `faith` · also `tensions`
**SIBLING:** `DS-FTH-2` owns the faith teaser and its constitutional gate; this block's sentence stands in for BOTH subsystems being quiet and is drawn only where war, treaty and faith are all dark.
**PROVENANCE + FENCE:** the purest example of the register in the product and the
one the corpus should generalize — a sentence that describes two absences
without apologizing for them and without hinting that content is being
withheld. Null war status is PEACE, not missing data, and the prose must say
peace. No variant may reference the tab, the page, the subscription, or the
existence of content elsewhere.
**PDF PARITY:** parity (the print chapter self-gates to null; when it renders at
all it renders this).

1. `[street]` This settlement is at peace and keeps no named faith.
2. `[ledger]` Nothing is being fought here and nothing is being kept here; the town's quarrels and its observances are both private matters.
3. `[visitor]` A stranger finds neither soldiers nor temples worth remarking on at {settlement}, and finds the town entirely unbothered by the absence of both.
4. `[elder]` {settlement} has neither a war nor a creed to its name at present, and has been in that condition long enough to have forgotten it is a condition.
5. `[counterforce]` There are quarrels {settlement} could be part of and observances it could keep, and it is part of none and keeps none.

### DS-WAR-4 — `PDF › liveWorld slice` · `campaign.worldState read through the same pure selectors + aggressionPosture`
> *the one band that exists only in print and on the war section*

**RECEIPT:** `src/pdf/lib/liveWorld.js:85-93` (`aggressionPosture`), `:118-347` · twin `WarFaithSection`
**STATE-KEY:** war / faith / rumors / beliefs / flow / pestilence / treaties
read through the SAME pure selectors the screen uses; self-gates to null when
nothing is live and no deity is projected · `aggressionPosture`
(`Belligerent` >1.18 · `Assertive` >1.04 · `Even-handed` · `Cautious` <0.96 ·
`Pacific` <0.82) — a band that exists ONLY here and in `WarFaithSection`, and
appears on no dossier tab at all.
**SLOTS:** `{settlement}` `{counterpart}` `{band}`
**SECTION-TARGET:** the printed chapter openers · also `tensions`
**PROVENANCE + FENCE:** `aggressionPosture` is a disposition, not a record. It
describes how the town's seat WOULD decide, and asserts nothing about what it
has decided; no variant may attach it to a specific war, muster or refusal. The
multiplier it comes from never reaches the page. Because the band appears on no
screen tab, these are the only cells in the cluster with no twin — authored
once, drawn in print and by `WarFaithSection`.
**PDF PARITY:** this IS the print surface.

**`Belligerent`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} reaches for force earlier than its neighbours do; where another hall would send a letter, this one weighs a muster first.
2. `[street]` The town's temper is known. When something goes wrong at a border, {settlement} is not the party that waits.
3. `[visitor]` A stranger negotiating with {settlement} finds the hall reaching for consequences early, and finds the reaching is not a bluff.

**`Assertive`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} presses its claims. It does not seek fights and it does not leave a grievance to settle itself, and the difference is legible in how it negotiates.
2. `[visitor]` A stranger dealing with {settlement} finds the hall firm rather than hostile, and finds that firmness holds when tested.
3. `[street]` The town at {settlement} does not let a thing go, and its neighbours settle small matters with it promptly rather than discover what happens otherwise.

**`Even-handed`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} weighs a quarrel the way most halls do — neither eager nor reluctant, and predictable to anybody who has dealt with it twice.
2. `[street]` The town has no reputation either way, which in these matters is itself a reputation.
3. `[visitor]` A stranger can predict what {settlement}'s hall will do about a grievance, which is the most useful thing a neighbour can say about a town.

**`Cautious`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} prefers not to. Where a grievance can be carried rather than pressed, this hall carries it, and the carrying is its habit rather than its calculation.
2. `[counterforce]` There are causes {settlement} holds and does not act on, and the holding is disposition rather than weakness.
3. `[street]` The town at {settlement} would rather be wronged than be at war, and says so when the question is put directly.

**`Pacific`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} will go a very long way to avoid a fight, and its neighbours price that in.
2. `[street]` The town does not answer provocation, and the not-answering is a settled policy rather than a mood.
3. `[counterforce]` {settlement} would not take up a cause another hall would seize on, and its neighbours can plan around that without having to test it.

### DS-WAR-5 — `WAR-ADJACENT — posture, siege, occupation, blockade, treaty burden, recovery` · `worldState.{warPosture,deployments,occupations,spatialLedgers.treaties} + regionalGraph war_front channels + activeConditions aftermath archetypes`
> *the whole war ladder in its own words, including the states that come after*

**RECEIPT:** `src/domain/display/mobilizationStatus.js:26-46` (`POSTURE_PHRASE`, `RAMP_ORDER`) · `src/domain/display/occupationStatus.js:36-72` (`STATE_PHRASE`, `STATE_PAYS`, `RESISTANCE_BANDS`) · `src/domain/display/warStatus.js:44-74` (the phantom-siege gate), `:283-298` · `src/domain/worldPulse/peaceTerms.js:164-200` · `src/domain/worldPulse/settlementPolitics.js:501-517` (the real threat read vs the dead fallback probes) · `src/domain/worldPulse/blockadeTransport.js:2-24` · `src/domain/activeConditions.js:307-467` (aftermath archetypes)
**STATE-KEY:** `warPosture[cid]{state, progress, covert?}` (seven, closed, ramp
order `peace → alert → war_preparation → mobilized`) · siege truth = a LIVE
`war_front` channel INTO the settlement, gated by `isLiveWarFront` ·
`occupations[occupiedId]{occupier, state, resistance, burden, benefitYield}`
(five rungs, closed) · `RESISTANCE_BANDS` (four, closed) · blockade impairment
stamped with the `stressor-blockade:` cause prefix and lifted when the blockade
ends · treaty term families with `burden01`, a duration in years, and
`stream: true` for installment terms · aftermath condition archetypes
(`siege_lifted` · `occupation_lifted` · `reconstruction` · `war_exhaustion` ·
`war_drain` · `war_spoils` · `relief_burden` · `trade_realignment`).
**SLOTS:** `{settlement}` `{counterpart}` `{route}` `{good}` `{term}` `{band}` `{timeband_since}` `{timeband_span}`
**SECTION-TARGET:** `tensions` · also `defense`
**SIBLING:** `DS-CND-1` owns the active-condition ladder at its own grain — severity, direction, provenance, duration and the grouped families — and authors `reconstruction` individually. The aftermath cells here are the PER-ARCHETYPE war reading of the same records; a composed page draws one grain, not both.
**PROVENANCE + FENCE (the phantom-siege trap, and it is the important one).** A
bare hostile relationship mints a `confirmed` front with **no army behind it**.
Siege prose rides `isLiveWarFront` and nothing else. Equally: the legacy
settlement-field probes — `underSiege`, `besieged`, `mobilizationState`,
`threatLevel` — are a tolerant FALLBACK that **no engine path writes**, so no
variant may source siege truth from them. `settlementWarStatus` returning null
is PEACE and gets its own authored cells rather than silence, because a page
that renders nothing where peace is has told the reader nothing. Occupation
PAYS from `extractive` upward; `contested` and `unstable` are a net drain on
the occupier, and that is a stated entailment of the rung rather than an
inference. `stream: true` is the only thing that licenses *still being paid*.
**PDF PARITY:** parity throughout (`liveWorld.js` reads the same selectors).

**posture `peace`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} is at peace and is arranged for it — nothing here is being held back for a war, and nothing is being produced against one.
2. `[street]` The town is doing what it does, and has been doing it long enough that a muster would be a genuine interruption.
3. `[visitor]` A stranger at {settlement} finds a town with no soldiers to speak of and no anxiety about the absence.

**posture `alert`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} is on alert. Nothing has been raised and everything is being watched, and the watching has begun to cost.
2. `[street]` The town has not changed what it does and has changed how carefully it does it.
3. `[unfolding]` The watching at {settlement} costs a little more each week and has not yet produced anything to watch, and the hall is beginning to be asked about that.

**posture `war_preparation`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} is gearing for war, with the economy beginning to shift onto a war footing; the change is visible in what is being bought before it is visible in who is being raised.
2. `[unfolding]` The town is turning, and the turning has not yet reached the point where it could be quietly turned back.
3. `[street]` The town at {settlement} is buying differently, storing differently and hiring differently, and none of it has been announced.

**posture `mobilized`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} is fully mobilized — a war economy, ready to march, and waiting on a decision rather than on preparation.
2. `[street]` The town is ready and is being kept ready, and being kept ready is expensive in ways the hall is discovering.
3. `[unfolding]` {settlement} is ready and idle, which is the most expensive condition a town can hold and the hardest to hold for long.

**posture `deployed`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement}'s army is in the field. What the town has is elsewhere, and what remains here is what could not be taken.
2. `[street]` The town is short of its own people and will be until the campaign ends, however it ends.
3. `[visitor]` A stranger finds {settlement} short of exactly the people a town this size should be full of, and does not have to ask where they are.

**posture `war_exhaustion`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} is war-weary and its army is spent; what could be raised here now is a fraction of what was raised, and the fraction is falling.
2. `[unfolding]` The town has reached the end of what it will give and has not formally said so, and the gap between those is where the next few seasons will be spent.
3. `[street]` The town at {settlement} has stopped answering the muster properly and has not formally refused it, and both facts are understood.

**posture `demobilizing`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} is standing down and the war economy is unwinding; what was made for the war is being sold, converted or abandoned, mostly the last.
2. `[street]` People are coming back to the town and are not returning to what they left, because what they left has been reorganized around their absence.
3. `[unfolding]` What {settlement} built for the war is being unbuilt at a loss, and the loss is arriving in the accounts a season after the fighting stopped.

**posture COVERT (any state)** *(every variant `dm-only`)* · **COMPOSITION FENCE (§0h V1-g):** `DS-WAR-1`'s covert-*mobilization* cell is the muster read; this is the *posture* read. Two fields, two rows, and **never both on one page** — a town that is quietly arming and quietly disposed toward war says one of these things, not two.
1. `[ledger · dm-only]` What {settlement} is doing about war it is doing without declaring, and the accounts have been arranged so that the doing does not appear.
2. `[street · dm-only]` Nothing at {settlement} looks like a town preparing for anything, and the looking-like-nothing has taken effort.
3. `[counterforce · dm-only]` {settlement}'s disposition has moved and its appearance has not, which took one decision at the top of the hall and a great many small silences beneath it.

**`settlementWarStatus` null (peace, not missing data)**
1. `[ledger]` There is no war at {settlement} — none against it and none of its own. The town's quarrels are the ordinary kind and are settled the ordinary way.
2. `[counterforce]` {settlement} has neighbours it disagrees with and no front with any of them; the disagreements have stayed disagreements.
3. `[street]` Nobody at {settlement} is fighting anybody, and the town would find the question of who odd rather than sensitive.

**live `war_front` INTO the settlement (a genuine siege)**
1. `[ledger]` A live front runs into {settlement}. There is an army here, it is arranged against the town, and the town's condition is now a question of stores and time.
2. `[street]` The gates are shut and there is something outside them, and the something is not a rumour.
3. `[unfolding]` What decides {settlement} now is being decided outside its walls, and nothing the town does inside them changes the arithmetic much.

**phantom front (hostility with no army behind it)**
1. `[counterforce]` {settlement} and {counterpart} are hostile and nothing is standing outside the walls; the enmity is real and no force has been committed to it.
2. `[ledger]` What is between {settlement} and {counterpart} is a relationship rather than a campaign, and the town has taken no siege measures because there is no siege.
3. `[street]` The town names {counterpart} as its enemy at {settlement} and has never seen a soldier of theirs, and both of those things are true at once.

**occupation `contested`**
1. `[ledger]` {counterpart} holds {settlement} and has not taken hold of it; the occupation is contested, and the garrison spends its strength on the town rather than through it.
2. `[street]` The soldiers are here and the town has not accepted that they are, and neither side has yet been able to change the other's mind.
3. `[unfolding]` The garrison at {settlement} spends its strength on holding the town rather than through it, and holding costs more every week it is not accepted.

**occupation `unstable`**
1. `[ledger]` {counterpart}'s control of {settlement} is precarious. It could be lost to an incident rather than to a campaign, and both sides are conscious of it.
2. `[unfolding]` Nothing about the occupation at {settlement} has settled, and every week it does not settle makes settling harder.
3. `[threshold]` The occupation at {settlement} sits at the point where a single incident decides it, and both the garrison and the town are behaving as though they know which incident.

**occupation `extractive`**
1. `[ledger]` {counterpart} bleeds {settlement} for everything the town holds. The occupation has begun to pay, and it pays by taking rather than by governing.
2. `[street]` What the town makes leaves it, and the leaving is thorough enough that the town has stopped planning past the season.
3. `[unfolding]` What {counterpart} takes from {settlement} it takes without investing anything, which pays now and forecloses everything later.

**occupation `stabilized`**
1. `[ledger]` {counterpart} holds {settlement} firmly. The occupation functions, yields more than it costs, and has the look of an arrangement rather than an event.
2. `[visitor]` A stranger at {settlement} finds the town working normally and takes a day or two to notice whose soldiers are standing in it.
3. `[street]` The town at {settlement} has arranged itself around the occupier and stopped calling it an occupation in ordinary conversation.

**occupation `vassalized`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} serves {counterpart} now. It is a client rather than a conquest, and the distinction is a real one to everybody except the people paying for it.
2. `[street]` The town belongs to {counterpart} now in the way a town belongs to anybody, and {settlement} has stopped conducting its affairs as though that might be temporary.
3. `[threshold]` {settlement}'s own institutions now execute {counterpart}'s decisions as their own, which is the difference between a conquest and a client.

**resistance `largely quiescent`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} is not resisting. The occupation meets no organized opposition, and what opposition exists is individual and unconnected.
2. `[street]` The town has decided, for now, that enduring costs less than the alternative.
3. `[visitor]` A stranger at {settlement} sees a town under somebody else's soldiers going about its business, and has to be told that is not normal.

**resistance `restive`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} is restive: sabotage and noncompliance harry the garrison steadily without ever amounting to a rising.
2. `[unfolding]` Nothing large is happening at {settlement} and a great many small things are, and the small things are getting less deniable.
3. `[threshold]` {settlement} is at the point where the small refusals have become a habit rather than a mood, and a habit is what a rising is assembled out of.

**resistance `simmering`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} simmers. The resistance is organized enough to be called that, and has not yet chosen the moment it is waiting for.
2. `[street]` The town is not quiet and is not in revolt, and everybody in it can feel which way that is trending.
3. `[unfolding]` What is organized at {settlement} is waiting rather than idle, and waiting is a decision that can be reversed in an afternoon.

**resistance `in open revolt`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} is in open revolt against {counterpart}. The occupation is being fought rather than endured, in the streets and in daylight.
2. `[unfolding]` What is happening at {settlement} will decide whether {counterpart} keeps it, and it will be decided within the season.
3. `[street]` The town at {settlement} is fighting the people holding it, in daylight, and has stopped calculating whether it can win.

**blockade stamped (recorded cause)**
1. `[ledger]` The transport into {settlement} is impaired and the impairment has a name on it: {counterpart}'s blockade, and nothing about the roads or the harbour themselves.
2. `[unfolding]` What reaches {settlement} reaches it around the blockade, in smaller quantities and at prices the town has not seen before.
3. `[street]` The town at {settlement} can name what is stopping its supply, and points at the water when it does.

**blockade LIFTED (the impairment is cleared)**
1. `[ledger]` The blockade on {settlement} has ended and what it impaired is running again; the town's supply has resumed, and what it lost while it was closed has not.
2. `[street]` The road is open at {settlement} and the prices have not come back down, and the town is learning which of those was actually the blockade's doing.
3. `[counterforce]` The blockade on {settlement} is gone and the town's condition has not returned with it, which tells the town something about what the blockade actually cost.

**a `stream: true` treaty term (the installment shape)**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} pays {counterpart} by the season under {term}, and the paying executes itself — nobody has to decide to do it, and stopping would be a decision.
2. `[street]` What the town owes goes out on a schedule, {timeband_span} of schedule remaining, and it is the bill of the war, still being paid.
3. `[unfolding]` The obligation at {settlement} executes itself each season, and stopping it would take a decision nobody in the hall has been willing to be the one to make.

**a HIGH-burden term**
1. `[ledger]` What {term} takes from {settlement} is a real share of what the town has, and the taking is the largest single fact in its accounts.
2. `[unfolding]` The burden {settlement} carries under {term} is heavy enough to be shaping what the town becomes rather than merely what it can afford.
3. `[street]` What the town sends away at {settlement} is enough that people plan their year around what is left, rather than around what they earn.

**aftermath `siege_lifted`**
1. `[street]` The army outside {settlement} has gone. The gates are open, the town is counting, and the counting is taking longer than anybody expected.
2. `[ledger]` The siege at {settlement} has been lifted and the town's condition has not yet caught up with the fact; stores, walls and people are all where the siege left them.
3. `[unfolding]` {settlement} is open again and is not yet what it was; the counting has started and nobody has said aloud how long the making-good will take.

**aftermath `occupation_lifted`**
1. `[ledger]` {counterpart}'s garrison has left {settlement}. What the occupation arranged remains arranged, and the town is now responsible for arrangements it did not choose.
2. `[street]` The soldiers are gone from {settlement} and the questions the town avoided while they were here have all arrived at once.
3. `[unfolding]` The arrangements {counterpart} made at {settlement} have outlived {counterpart}'s presence, and the town now has to decide which of them to keep.


**aftermath `war_exhaustion`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} is carrying the mark of the fighting it did. Nothing here is broken, and nothing here has the strength it had.
2. `[street]` The town has not recovered and has stopped talking about recovering, which is a stage rather than a mood.
3. `[unfolding]` {settlement} is not recovering so much as levelling off, and the level is below where the town started.

**aftermath `war_drain`**
1. `[ledger]` The war is still taking from {settlement} after the fighting — the costs did not end where the campaign did, and the town is meeting them out of ordinary revenue.
2. `[unfolding]` What {settlement} spends on a war that is over is spending it cannot spend on anything else, and the list of anything else is getting longer.
3. `[street]` The town is still paying for a war that is over at {settlement}, and has begun to resent the paying in a way it never resented the fighting.

**aftermath `war_spoils`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} came out of the fighting with more than it went in with, and the more is in the town — visible, unevenly distributed, and generating its own quarrels.
2. `[street]` The town profited, and the profit is not shared in the proportions anybody expected before the fighting started.
3. `[unfolding]` What {settlement} took is being turned into something permanent by the people who hold it, and the town's shape after that will not be the shape it had.

**aftermath `relief_burden`**
1. `[ledger]` {settlement} is carrying people it did not have before; the relief the town gave is now a standing cost, and the cost has outlasted the crisis that produced it.
2. `[unfolding]` What was generosity at {settlement} has become arithmetic, and the town has begun to say so out loud.
3. `[street]` The people {settlement} took in are still here, and the town's generosity has become a standing item that somebody has to fund.

**aftermath `trade_realignment`**
1. `[ledger]` The routes out of {settlement} are not the routes that were; the war moved where things go, and where things go has not moved back.
2. `[street]` The stalls at {settlement} are quieter than the town remembers them being, and the carters who used to fill them have somewhere else to be now.
3. `[elder]` {settlement} trades with different towns than it did {timeband_since}, and the change was not chosen by anybody here.

### W2 → THE PARITY DECISION TABLE (a continuation of `DS-GEN-10`'s, not a fork)

Every pool this writer mints declares whether the PDF chapter renders **the
same bytes** (parity) or is **print-deferred**. The default is parity, because a
printed dossier that says less than the screen is the failure mode the parity
contract exists to prevent. Each row is vetoable.

| Shape | PDF chapter | Decision | Reason |
|---|---|---|---|
| `DS-POW-1` legitimacy banner | power | **PARITY** | `viewModel.js:44-49,564-568` already maps `publicLegitimacy` and `LEGITIMACY_FACTOR_LABELS`; the band and the dominant factor are both in the slice |
| `DS-POW-2` stability + authority | power | **PARITY** | the governing faction and the stability string are both in the power slice |
| `DS-POW-3` the ladder | — | **PRINT-DEFERRED** | the rung mirror is a `townMap` read the PDF view-model does not reach; deferring is honest and inventing a read is not |
| `DS-POW-4` rule and succession | power | **PARITY** | `coupContenders` is a pure derivation over the same settlement object |
| `DS-POW-5` ruling structure + lens | power, economics | **PARITY** | the lens is a pure function of `economicBase` × governing archetype, both already sliced |
| `DS-POW-6` the residual ladder cells | power | **PARITY**, except the seat-rank cells | the seat-rank cells are `dm-only` and the print chapter carries no audience gate of its own |
| `DS-POW-7` blocs and the divided court | — | **PRINT-DEFERRED** | `politicsLedgers` is world state `liveWorld.js` does not carry (it slices war, faith, rumors, beliefs, flow, pestilence and treaties, and not politics) |
| `DS-DEF-1` defensive posture header | defense | **PARITY** | readiness, terrain and strategic value are all in the defense slice |
| `DS-DEF-2` the five threat rows | defense | **PARITY** | `buildThreatAssessment` is a pure dependency-free leaf both surfaces already call |
| `DS-DEF-3` public order banner | defense | **PARITY** | including the compound override, which the print chapter must honour identically or the paper contradicts the screen |
| `DS-DEF-4` criminal structure + capture | defense | **PARITY** for structure; **PRINT-DEFERRED** at `corrupted` and above | same audience-gate reason as `DS-POW-6` |
| `DS-DEF-5` forces and fortifications | defense | **PARITY** | institution presence is frozen profile state |
| `DS-DEF-6` supporting capabilities | defense | **PARITY**, except the blockade-bypass cells | the magical bypass is live-world state the print chapter self-gates away from |
| `DS-DEF-7` live readiness + war front | defense | **PARITY** for the bands; **PRINT-DEFERRED** for the contributor cells | `causalState` contributors are live and per-tick; the band is stable enough to print |
| `DS-DEF-8` active military status | defense | **PARITY** | `DEFENSE_STRESS_STATUS` is already the shared pdf-4 set — this row is the reason it was derived over the producer table |
| `DS-DEF-9` magic dependency | viability | **PARITY** | a boolean on the frozen profile; the twin `OverviewTab` badge draws the same pool per R-DST-W2-c |
| `DS-DEF-10` arms, badges, postures | defense | **PARITY** | frozen ladder plus the shared posture set |
| `DS-WAR-1` the war block | liveWorld | **PARITY** | `liveWorld.js:118-347` carries the same selector phrases verbatim |
| `DS-WAR-2` the treaty block | liveWorld | **PARITY** | `renderAllTreaties` is imported directly by the print slice |
| `DS-WAR-3` whole-tab dormant note | liveWorld | **PARITY** | the slice self-gates to null; where it renders at all, this is what it renders |
| `DS-WAR-4` aggression posture | liveWorld | **PRINT-NATIVE** | the only band in the cluster with no screen tab — authored once, drawn by the print slice and by `WarFaithSection`, and by nothing else |
| `DS-WAR-5` the war-adjacent ladders | liveWorld | **PARITY** | the print slice reads the same selectors the screen does |

**ONE WIRING CONSTRAINT, recorded because it is not a content question.**
`foodCore()` re-derives food behind the `canonicalViewModel` flag, so the print
chapter can in principle read a different food band from the screen. Any W2 pool
whose prose depends on a food band — `DS-POW-1`'s food-led legitimacy cells,
`DS-DEF-2`'s `Disasters & Famine` row, `DS-DEF-6`'s `Logistics & Supply` row —
must draw its band from the **same** derivation the surface it prints beside
used, or the printed dossier will disagree with the screen about whether the
town can eat.

## WRITER-3 — CLUSTER: FAITH + STRESSORS + POPULATION

**Eight state shapes, 467 authored variants across 140 angle-distinct pools as merged** (422 authored here, plus the 45 folded in from `DS-GEN-4` at the merge — §0h V1-a), beside **1 existing string
recorded canonical-at-zero** rather than rewritten (the faith teaser's
true-neutral line — see R-DST-C). Three surface blocks (`DS-STR-1`, `DS-FTH-1`,
`DS-FTH-2`) framing what the Overview crisis banner and the Faith panel actually
render, and five ladder blocks (`DS-STR-2`, `DS-CND-1`, `DS-FTH-3`, `DS-POP-1`,
`DS-POP-2`) reading the closed vocabularies behind them. Non-redundancy between
the two kinds is R-DST-A; each block names its sibling where they touch.

**Adopted whole, not restated per block:** §0a's scope, fences and Law One ·
§0b's register, woven test and angle palette · §0c's slots · §0d's bands and the
digit ban · §0e's audience marking · §0f's resolutions.

### W3-a CLUSTER-LOCAL SLOTS (two, declared here, minted nowhere else)

§0c carries no proper-noun slot for a faith. `{faction}` is a political actor
and cannot hold a creed; `{institution}` holds the *building*, not the belief.

| Slot | Fills with | Why it is needed |
|---|---|---|
| `{creed}` | the deity or faith by its authored name, from `primaryDeitySnapshot.name` / the `deities[ref]` entry | faith's proper noun; the patron line, the standing line and the fall line all need it named |
| `{rival_creed}` | the opposing, rising or contesting creed | the schism, the fall and the pressed-seat pools each need BOTH creeds named |

A temple renders through §0c's `{institution}`; a devotional occasion renders
through `{season}` or a `{timeband_*}` fill. This block declares no other slot.

### W3-b THE CLUSTER'S ENTAILMENT LEDGER (six constraints, each with its receipt)

R-DST-B is the file's central law and this cluster carries six sharpenings of
it. A variant that violates one is a defect, not a stylistic preference.

| # | Constraint | Receipt |
|---|---|---|
| E-1 | **The unnamed attacker stays unnamed.** A stressor at `originContext.variant: unattributed` carries `attackerSettlementId: null` and `attackerLabel: null` BY DESIGN. No variant may confabulate a besieger, a sponsor or a raider host — the not-knowing is the state. | `stressorDynamics.js:790-800` |
| E-2 | **No forecast vocabulary in population prose, ever.** Every population reading is retrospective — what the ring HAS done. A source-scan pin forbids forecast words entering that table and this corpus inherits the ban. `[threshold]` may state an edge in this cluster but never a crossing. | `trendLens.js:21-30`; pin `tests/domain/trendLensClaimsParity.test.js` |
| E-3 | **No invented motion in condition prose.** A condition whose input `status` is missing, legacy or `'active'` holds FLAT — the drift reads the raw status precisely so canonical defaulting cannot invent a direction. Direction prose draws only where a valid directional status is written. | `activeConditions.js:885-900` |
| E-4 | **LAW ONE, faith's hardest reading.** The engine models BELIEVERS — shares, legitimacy, piety, congregations — never gods. Rank is a count of seats, not a claim about a god. A fall is what the believers did and the reason they gave. A reading is what an observance said, never what a god did. | `DESIGN_FP_FAITH.md:46-52` |
| E-5 | **The latent pantheon is never named.** The teaser fires exactly where `primaryDeitySnapshot` is absent — which is also where a latent pantheon may exist and be stripped. `faithPanelModel` by construction never reads `config.latentPantheon`. No teaser variant may hint at, tease or characterise an unrevealed deity, and every teaser variant must stay TRUE if the seed never reveals one. | `faithPanelModel.js:11-15`; `FaithSection.jsx:221-263` |
| E-6 | **Do not double-tell the exodus.** The M4 rumor lane already emits a `migration_flight` line off the SAME `spatialLedgers.migration` columns. Both surfaces are honest; the composer must not draw `DS-POP-1`'s migration pool into a page already carrying the rumor. | `demographicsHerald.js:31-38` |

**Two inheritances recorded because they bind this cluster from outside it.**
The Overview's **FROZEN-VS-LIVE** carve-out — the five score bars and the
Viability and Defense statuses are first-survey verdicts with no pulse
writeback, and only Food Security is re-judged (`OverviewTab.jsx:177`) — means
**no stressor or condition variant in this block may claim a score bar moved.**
And *"slider"* is UI vocabulary that has already leaked into world prose once
(`RelationshipsTab.jsx:195-200`); nothing here repeats it.

**R-DST-B, applied to this cluster's fields.** A **stressor** is an event record
— `originContext`, `lifecycleStage` and the synergy companions are event
provenance, so historical clauses ARE licensed in `DS-STR-2`. A **condition**
licenses a historical clause only through `causes[]` or
`triggeredAt.sourceEventType`, which is why `DS-CND-1` splits its cause pool
from its description pool. A **faith band** — `standing`, `legitimacy`,
`piety.band` — carries **no provenance at all** and gets plain description; the
licences in `DS-FTH-3` are `piety.causes[]`, `patronFalls[].cause` and
`deities[ref].tenure`, and nothing else. A **population trend band** is a
derived reading over a ring and carries **no provenance whatsoever**; the
licences in `DS-POP-1` are the demographic receipt and the migration ledger.

---

### DS-STR-1 — Overview › Active crisis banners · `settlement.stress[] {type, icon, label, colour, summary, crisisHook}`

**STATE-KEY.** `stress[].type` — the fifteen closed generator stress types
(`STRESS_TYPE_MAP`) — × banner arity (one, several).

**ENTAILMENT.** The banner today prints the generator's `summary` and
`crisisHook` under an ACTIVE CRISIS chip; the dossier never says in its own voice
what the stressor is doing to the town. This block is that missing voice and it
**FRAMES** the generator strings, never restates them (§0a). The type is its own
provenance — a `famine` row licenses *"the shortfall is public now"* — and
licenses nothing further: no attacker, no creditor identity, no score movement
(FROZEN-VS-LIVE). Sibling: `DS-STR-2` reads the stressor's lifecycle, counterforce
and origin; this block reads the type as the town experiences it. A composed page
draws at most one of each.

**FOLDED IN (VERIFIER-1, §0h V1-a).** `DS-GEN-4` authored this same surface over the same fifteen closed types and is now a pointer to this block; its variants are carried in whole as entries 4–6 of each pool, and its provenance ruling is adopted verbatim below. Six variants per type is the correct depth here rather than a luxury: the Overview banner is among the highest-firing surfaces in the corpus (R-DST-W4-i's cadence rung).

**PROVENANCE (adopted from `DS-GEN-4`).** **PARTIAL, AND BY WORD ONLY.** Seven of the fifteen type words name a *transition* and license exactly that transition and nothing else — `recently_betrayed`, `occupied`, `plague_onset`, `mass_migration`, `slave_revolt`, `succession_void`, `religious_conversion`. The other eight name a *condition* and get plain description: `famine`, `indebted`, `politically_fractured`, `infiltrated`, `monster_pressure`, `insurgency`, `wartime`, `under_siege`. **No variant names an agent the record does not hold** — `under_siege` entails a besieger and does not entail *who*, so no besieger is described. `[elder]` unavailable per R-DST-W4-b.

**SLOTS.** `{settlement}` `{season}` `{band}`

**UNDER SIEGE** · `under_siege`
1. `[visitor]` Nothing moves through the gates. {settlement} lives on what is already inside the walls, and counts it daily.
2. `[ledger]` The walls are held from outside; the fields beyond them have not been worked since the lines closed.
3. `[street]` Trade stops at the gate rather than the market now — the siege decides what the town eats.
4. `[street]` {settlement} is shut, and the shutting is not the town's decision; every arrangement inside the walls has been rebuilt around the gates staying closed.
5. `[ledger]` {settlement}'s stores are being counted daily and issued against a list, and the list is shorter than the town.
6. `[visitor]` Nobody goes in or out of {settlement}, and the walls have people on them at hours walls do not normally have people on them.

**FAMINE** · `famine`
1. `[street]` There is not enough to eat, and the shortfall is public now rather than private — the granary answers fewer hands each week.
2. `[unfolding]` Hunger has moved from the poorest streets into the ordinary ones; what the fields returned does not reach the table.
3. `[ledger]` The market has grain, and the price of it has become a kind of politics; families are eating stores meant for {season}.
4. `[street]` There is not enough food in {settlement} and there is no longer a polite way to discuss it.
5. `[ledger]` What {settlement}'s granary holds is being issued by rule rather than by price, and the rule is being tested.
6. `[visitor]` {settlement}'s market is open and thin, and the thinness is not seasonal.

**UNDER OCCUPATION** · `occupied`
1. `[visitor]` Another power's writ runs here. The offices are the same offices; the authority behind them is not.
2. `[ledger]` Revenue leaves before it is spent — {settlement} is administered, and the administration is not its own.
3. `[street]` The garrison is quartered inside the walls, and the town's business is conducted around it.
4. `[street]` {settlement} is held by somebody it did not choose, and the town's own hall now advises rather than decides.
5. `[ledger]` Every licence, levy and permission in {settlement} passes a hand that is not the town's before it takes effect.
6. `[visitor]` {settlement} functions and it functions under supervision, and the supervision is not disguised.

**POLITICALLY FRACTURED** · `politically_fractured`
1. `[ledger]` Nothing is decided. The factions each hold enough to block and none hold enough to rule, so the work of governing sits unattended.
2. `[unfolding]` Authority here is divided past the point of use — repairs, judgments and appointments all wait on a consensus that does not arrive.
3. `[street]` The town is governed in pieces, and the pieces do not agree; deferral has become the only policy anyone can pass.
4. `[street]` {settlement} has no single answer to anything, because there is no longer anyone entitled to give one.
5. `[ledger]` Two sets of instructions circulate in {settlement} and the clerks have quietly decided which to obey.
6. `[visitor]` Asking {settlement} a simple question gets a careful one back, because the answer depends on who is asking for whom.

**INDEBTED TO AN OUTSIDE POWER** · `indebted`
1. `[ledger]` A share of everything {settlement} earns leaves for a creditor beyond the walls, and the share is not small.
2. `[counterforce]` The books balance only because nothing is being built — the debt is served first and the town second.
3. `[visitor]` The obligation is outside and the payment is here; coin that would have gone to the works goes to the collector.
4. `[ledger]` {settlement}'s obligations exceed what its year produces, and the shortfall is being met by promising against the next one.
